# Iraq — English books

**IRAQ OPENED 2026-11-03 under the user's
all-closed-countries ruling.** Iraq is CLOSED in the
library. Its English set as recorded is 34 files —
Grades 1–12, 22 student-side + 12 teacher's books, all
in hand, no link-only books — and all 34 were pulled
from the secured GitHub library (repo tree + the
mid-size-books / full-quality-oversized-books
releases), 940 MB, 5,457 PDF pages. Every book below
was read whole and citation-checked as a separate
pass; the anchor total is stated in the closing note
of this header once the final sections are spliced in.

The course is one Garnet series, *English for Iraq*,
written for Iraq's Ministry of Education (General
Directorate of Curricula): primary G1–G6 (1st–6th
Primary), intermediate G7–G9 (1st–3rd Intermediate),
preparatory G10–G12. Primary runs a combined
Pupil + Activity Book at G1–G2 and separate Student's
Book + Activity Book from G3; every grade G1–G12 has
its Teacher's Book. The intermediate Student's Books
as held are only **Book One** of two-book sets — see
the corrections.

fixes owed):**

## Grade 1 — English for Iraq, 1st Primary, Pupil and Activity Book (combined pupil + activity book)

### Identity block

- **Identity verdict:** The file matches the record title ("English for Iraq — Pupil and Activity Book", Grade 1). One bound volume containing the colour Pupil Book (printed pp. 1–88), a black-and-white Activities (workbook) section (printed pp. 89–161) and cut-out Word cards (printed pp. 163–176).
- **Title as printed:** *English for Iraq* — "1st Primary" — "Pupil and Activity Book" (front cover, PDF p. 1; repeated on the title page, PDF p. 3).
- **Authors as printed:** Jenny Quintana and Helen O'Neill (cover and title page). The title page also credits an Editorial and Adaptation Committee (Ministry of Education English Curriculum Committee).
- **Publisher:** Garnet Publishing Ltd (imprint: "Garnet Education"), 8 Southern Court, South Street, Reading RG1 4QS, UK (imprint, printed p. 2).
- **Edition / year as printed:** "First edition, copyright © 2013 Garnet Publishing Ltd." Production code printed on the imprint page: IRQ13 PB 1stP.
- **ISBN as printed:** No ISBN is printed anywhere in the book — the imprint page carries none, and the back cover carries only a stock/library sticker ("TKGD002684"), not an ISBN barcode.
- **PDF pages:** 179.
- **Printed vs PDF offset:** Printed page = PDF page − 2 throughout the numbered book. PDF p. 1 = front cover, PDF p. 2 = blank inside cover, PDF p. 3 = title page, PDF p. 4 = printed p. 2 (imprint + Icon Key), printed p. 3 (PDF p. 5) = Book map, numbered content runs to printed p. 161 (PDF p. 163); PDF p. 164 (printed p. 162) is a blank divider; the Word cards (book map: p. 163) are unnumbered, PDF pp. 165–178; PDF p. 179 = back cover. All page references below are printed pages.
- **Text-layer character:** No usable text layer (the PDF's embedded text is effectively empty — one byte per page); the book is a clean colour scan. Read whole from 4-up contact sheets of page renders.
- **Cast / design:** Two robot mascots — Robbie (tall) and Rosie (small) — teach alongside Iraqi children Ahmed and Nada, with Hani, Iman and Sami, a teacher, clowns and a friendly monster. Every pupil lesson follows one pattern: a letter banner with two or three example words, a large picture scene with short speech bubbles, and a stroke-order letter-formation strip. Task types are signalled by a printed Icon Key (printed p. 2): listen/look/say, look, match, count, trace, do, and similar.

### Unit-by-unit summary

**Front matter (printed pp. 2–5).** Imprint and Icon Key (p. 2); the Book map (p. 3), which lists all eight units with their alphabet words, vocabulary and language, plus Stories (p. 74), Picture dictionary (p. 86), Activities (p. 89) and Word cards (p. 163); then a two-page alphabet picture spread (pp. 4–5) showing one picture word for every letter a–z.

**Unit 1 — My house (pp. 6–15).** Ten lessons introduce the letters a–f, each with its example words (a: ant, apple; b: ball, bus, bird; c: car, cat, cake; d: dog, duck; e: egg, elephant; f: flower, fish), with letter tracing on every page. The scenes carry the book's first language: greetings (Hello / Goodbye), introductions (What's your name? — I'm Nada / I'm Ahmed), Who's this?, and the politeness pair Here you are. / Thank you. Colours red, blue, yellow and green appear in a balloon scene; the unit closes with a big farm picture for finding and counting the unit's words.

**Unit 2 — My school (pp. 16–25).** Letters g–l (girl, goat; horse, house; ink, ice-cream; jelly, juice, jumper; key, kite; lemon, lion) and the classroom-object set (bag, bin, book, CD, chair, desk, pen, pencil, pencil case, rubber, ruler) labelled in a classroom scene. Language: classroom instructions (Stand up. Sit down. Hands up. Hands down. Turn around.), the actions Open / Draw / Close, and the identification exchange What's this? — Is it a pen? — No. — Is it a pencil? — Yes. A spot-the-difference classroom pair and a closing "Who's in the bus?" picture (people vs animals) round out the unit.

**Unit 3 — My toys (pp. 26–35).** Letters m–t (monkey, moon; net, nose; orange, olive; plane, penguin, pizza; question, queen; robot, radio, rabbit; sweet, snake, sun; taxi, tiger, tomato), the toy set (bear, bike, doll, skates, toy, video game, yo-yo, plus balloon and present) and numbers 1–10, taught by counting balloons and domino-style dot cards. Language: How many toys? and the age exchange How old are you? — I'm 6 / I'm 4, set at a birthday-party scene; a toy-shop picture is used for counting practice.

**Unit 4 — Revision (pp. 36–39).** A four-lesson revision unit that completes the alphabet with u–z (umbrella, uniform; van, violin; window, watch; x-ray, yo-yo, zebra) while recycling earlier language in games: Is it a bus? — Yes., What can you see? over a party-table picture, and How many vans can you see? No new topic vocabulary beyond the u–z example words.

**Unit 5 — At the zoo (pp. 40–49).** No new letters; the alphabet is now used for word-level work. New vocabulary: zoo animals (camel, spider, zebra, added to the recycled elephant, tiger, lion, monkey, penguin, goat, horse, rabbit, fish, snake), the colours black, brown, grey and white, and the adjectives big, funny, little. Lessons practise counting animals in grids (How many red birds?), matching pictures to whole written words (penguin, monkey, tiger, elephant, camel), a wordless spider picture story, and location language: Where's the blue tiger? — In the bag, with in/on. The final lesson (p. 49) is the book's first page of full reading sentences built on There is … / There are … (e.g. a dog in a bus; three cats; an ant on a bag).

**Unit 6 — My body (pp. 50–59).** Parts of the body (ears, eyes, feet, hair, hands, head, mouth, nose, toes, plus arms and body) introduced through face-painting and clown pictures with the frame My ears / My nose / My eyes. Description language follows: children identify clowns and build monsters from described parts (I have three red ears), culminating in a first-person monster description combining body parts, numbers and colours (a blue body, two green heads, three red arms, a yellow leg). Washing routines give I wash my face / hands / feet / hair, and the unit ends with a guessing game (What is it? — a blue body and pink feet — It's a monkey!).

**Unit 7 — My family (pp. 60–69).** Family words (brother, dad, grandma, grandpa, mum, sister) on a family-photo page, then the house set (bedroom, computer, door, garden, hall, kitchen, sitting room, stairs, table, TV, wall, roof, plus bed, chair, desk, bin) explored through a two-page cutaway house, a bedroom spot-the-difference pair and a wordsearch. Language: It's … for identifying people and robots (It's my friend. It's Rosie.), How old are you? recycled, and I have … with colour + vehicle (a yellow taxi, a blue car, a green van). The closing lesson is the counting song page "1 little, 2 little, 3 little elephants … 10 little elephants in a line."

**Unit 8 — Revision (pp. 70–73).** Four closing revision lessons in a different, flatter art style: a four-frame story "Nada's little dog" (It's my little dog; a jack-in-the-box joke), spot-the-difference pairs, I have a pen / I have a pen and a pencil built up item by item, and a "My bedroom" drawing page on which Robbie and Rosie say Goodbye.

**Stories (pp. 74–85).** Six two-page picture stories with speech-bubble captions only, each recycling one unit's language: "Nada's birthday" (Units 3–4: balloons, presents, Happy birthday, Nada; It's an elephant!; Thank you.), "In the toy shop" (There are lots of dolls; Come and play!; You can have 1 toy; What do you want?), "At the zoo" (greetings to monkeys, rabbits and elephants; the lion frightens Robbie), "Ahmed and Nada's monster" (building a monster with a clown; counting ears and toes; I'm a silly monster!), "At the circus" (Look at the dogs / monkeys / boys!; a custard-pie slapstick ending with I'm sorry.), and "Sami's rocket" (It's the moon!; It's my rocket.; a dream flight to a moon with No toys!).

**Picture dictionary (pp. 86–88).** The book's alphabet words a–z as picture–word pairs (pp. 86–87), ending with a Colours page (p. 88) naming ten colours: red, blue, yellow, green, orange, pink, black, white, brown, grey.

**Activities (pp. 89–161).** The black-and-white workbook half of the volume, one or more pages per pupil lesson for all eight units plus "Extra activities" pages for Units 4 and 8. Early work is pre-writing: tracing dotted lines and letter shapes, colouring named items, matching letters to pictures, odd-one-out and listen-and-tick tasks. It progresses through copying whole words, number tracing 1–10 (Unit 3), wordsearches and initial-letter completion, to — in Unit 5 — trace-and-copy of whole vocabulary words, and in Unit 6 the introduction of CAPITAL letters A–Z (formation strips, upper↔lower matching, copying the characters' names). Units 7–8 add short read-and-colour sentence work (A blue van; Wall 1 is blue. The door is yellow.; I have a red ball; I have 2 green pencils and a pink ruler), story-ordering, join-the-dots over the capital alphabet, and word copying throughout.

**Word cards (pp. 163–176; PDF pp. 165–178).** Cut-out card sheets (printed landscape): lower- and upper-case letter cards, number cards 1–10 with the number words, colour-splash and colour-word cards, and a full set of vocabulary word cards with matching picture cards covering every unit's word list, ending with classroom and house words (pen, table, bin, park, toys, zoo, wall, desk, bed, chair, door, clown, pencil case, bag, pencil, rubber).

### Grammar spine

Entirely formulaic, oral-first frames with no paradigms or rules stated: greetings and introductions (Hello / Goodbye; What's your name? — I'm …); identification (What's this? / What is it? — It's a …; Is it a …? — Yes / No); classroom imperatives (Stand up, Sit down, Open, Draw, Close, Turn around); quantity (How many …? with numbers 1–10; How old are you? — I'm …); possession (I have … + colour + noun; My … for body parts); location (Where's the …? — in/on; There is … / There are … in the final pupil lesson of Unit 5); and description by adjective + noun (colour, big, funny, little) including monster descriptions with numbers of body parts. The Activities add the written forms of the same frames and, from Unit 6, capital-letter formation.

### Honest negatives

- **No phonics as a system:** letters are taught one by one with example words and tracing; there is no blending, no CVC word-building and no decoding strand — reading stays at whole-word recognition of the taught vocabulary.
- **No numbers beyond 10**; no days, months, seasons, weather or telling the time; no food vocabulary beyond party items met inside other units (cake, jelly, pizza, sweets, ice-cream); no clothes set beyond jumper and uniform.
- **No past or future forms, no present continuous, no pronouns beyond I/my/you,** no possessive 's in the taught frames (it appears only in story titles such as "Nada's birthday"), and no question forms beyond the memorised frames listed above.
- **Capital letters never appear in the Pupil Book lessons** — they are introduced only in the Activities section (Unit 6, printed pp. 136–141).
- **Writing is tracing and copying only:** single letters, then words, then (Activities, Units 7–8) short copied phrases; there is no free writing, and no test or assessment section.
- **The book is English-only** — no Arabic glossing anywhere, including the Picture dictionary.
- Copy defects: none structural — the scan is complete and in order, including both covers. A few Activities pages of this scanned copy carry a pupil's faint pencil completions (e.g. traced numbers on printed p. 116); printed p. 162 is blank by design (divider before the Word cards).

## Grade 1 — English for Iraq, 1st Primary, Teacher's Book (teacher's guide)

### Identity block

- **Identity verdict:** The file is the genuine Grade 1 teacher's book for the same course as the Pupil and Activity Book, but two record details need correcting: the book's own printed kind is **"Teacher's Book"** (not "Teacher's Guide"), and it has **three authors**, not two — the record should be checked against the cover credits below.
- **Title as printed:** *English for Iraq* — "1st Primary" — "Teacher's Book" (front cover, PDF p. 1; title page, PDF p. 3).
- **Authors as printed:** Jenny Quintana, Helen O'Neill **and Sarah MacBurnie** (cover and title page). The Pupil and Activity Book credits only Quintana and O'Neill; MacBurnie is an additional author of the Teacher's Book. The title page also credits the Editorial and Adaptation Committee (Ministry of Education English Curriculum Committee).
- **Publisher:** Garnet Publishing Ltd (Garnet Education), 8 Southern Court, South Street, Reading RG1 4QS, UK (imprint, PDF p. 4).
- **Edition / year as printed:** "First edition, copyright © 2013 Garnet Publishing Ltd." Production code: IRQ13 TB 1stP.
- **ISBN as printed:** No ISBN printed anywhere in the book; the back cover carries only a stock/library sticker ("TKGU02685").
- **PDF pages:** 220.
- **Printed vs PDF offset:** Printed page = PDF page − 4. PDF p. 1 = cover, PDF p. 2 = blank, PDF p. 3 = title page, PDF p. 4 = imprint (unnumbered), PDF p. 5 = printed p. 1 (Contents), and the last numbered page is printed p. 213 (List of picture cards, PDF p. 217); PDF pp. 218–219 are blank and PDF p. 220 is the back cover. All page references below are printed pages.
- **Text-layer character:** The PDF is a greyscale scan with a partial, badly garbled embedded OCR layer on only five pages (imprint, contents and three others); unusable as a text. Read whole from 4-up contact sheets of page renders.
- **Design / language:** Black-and-white throughout. The Introduction and the Unit 1 teaching notes are fully **bilingual**: every English page is followed by its Arabic translation page (the Arabic pages also carry the lesson word lists glossed into Arabic). From Unit 2 onward the notes are English-only, and each unit's notes end with a blank page (pp. 91, 115, 127, 147, 167, 185, 195). Course components named in the book: Pupil and Activity Book (PAB), Teacher's Book, MP3 audio (97 lesson tracks), a Teacher Resource File audio (Tracks 98–110), picture/word, number and colour cards, and four A2 posters (Poster 1 lower-case alphabet; Poster 2 colours and numbers; Poster 3 upper- and lower-case alphabet; Poster 4 house and family scene).

### Section-by-section summary

**Contents (p. 1).** Introduction (pp. 2–19), Course map (pp. 20–21), Teaching Notes for Units 1–8 (pp. 22–195), Appendices: Transcripts (pp. 196–210), Teacher Resource File Transcript (pp. 211–212), List of picture cards (p. 213).

**Introduction (pp. 2–19, English with Arabic translation pages interleaved).** A course rationale and method guide: the Pupil and Activity Book and its Activities second half; the stories (one topic-linked story per unit except Unit 1); mini picture/word cards; the course structure (eight units, 68 lessons; Units 4 and 8 are revision); the six characters (Ahmed, his sister Nada, friends Hani and Iman, robots Robbie and Rosie); lesson structure (receptive skills before productive); the eight aims of the course (engagement and confidence first; introducing the sound of English through recordings; naming people and objects; simple sentences; basic reading "using phonics and whole word recognition"; first handwriting steps); a long section on teaching very young children (emotional, physical, educational and linguistic characteristics and their classroom implications); guidance on listening (four text types), speaking, pronunciation, vocabulary recycling, "talking about pictures", reading activities, the presentation of letter sounds (a "board" held by Robbie and Rosie, one letter and its key words at a time), pre-writing and the three "ready-to-write" steps (sit correctly, hold the pencil correctly, position the book correctly — with a note for left-handers); Total Physical Response teaching, including the commands game **Salman Says**; procedures for songs, rhymes and stories (including "Talk about the pictures in Arabic"); "The classroom" (atmosphere, organisation, discipline, routines, and a list of essential classroom language — Sit down, please; Stand up, please; Ssh! Quietly, please; Hands up!; Come here, please; Listen; Listen again; and the longer list on pp. 18–19); and assessment: there is **no formal assessment** in the early primary grades — teachers keep a progress chart per child (confidence, interest, interaction, behaviour) and evaluate their own lessons with five set questions.

**Course map (pp. 20–21).** A two-page table giving, for each of the eight units, its Aims and Activities in parallel columns — the fastest accurate summary of the year's intended progression (familiarity with the sound of English and left-to-right direction in Unit 1, through blending and sentence copying by Units 6–8).

**Teaching notes — pattern (pp. 22–195).** Each unit opens with Objectives grouped as Listening / Speaking / Pre-reading / Pre-writing, then a Word List by lesson (an asterisk marks receptive-only items; Unit 1's list is also given in an Arabic-glossed version). Each of the 68 lessons then gets an Aims / Language / Materials box and numbered step-by-step procedures with the audio transcripts printed inline, plus "Additional" boxes (Extend / Support activities, games, homework setting). Recurring procedures: the greetings routine (Hello. How are you today? — Hello. I'm fine, thank you.), letter introduction with a mime for each letter sound, choral drilling from the tracks, the tracing routine, card games (What's missing?, Pairs, I Spy, Chinese Whispers, Kim's Game, Feely Bag, Alphabetical Order), and a set homework activity from the Activities section almost every lesson.

**Unit 1 notes (pp. 22–69, bilingual).** Letters a–f and colours as in the pupil book, but the notes teach more language than the pupil pages print: How are you today? / I'm fine, thank you from Lesson 6; What's his/her name? (receptive); The Alphabet Song (Track 12) and the What's Your Name? song (Track 11); The Colour Song (Track 19); orange and pink and What's your favourite colour? in Lesson 9; the plural –s note (pencil/pencils) in Lesson 9; and the note that the book avoids he/she, him/her, his/her by using plural pronouns. Lesson 10 adds the animal-sounds listening track (Track 20).

**Unit 2 notes — "At school" (pp. 70–91).** (The Teacher's Book heads this unit "At school"; the pupil book's map calls it "My school".) Letters g–l and classroom objects; an action rhyme (Hands up! Hands down! …, Track 22); the song Off To School (Track 26); Good morning and a short classroom conversation (Track 31); a told story about Robbie at school (Track 32, the elephant outside the window); the guessing exchange Is it a (pen)? built up in Lesson 7 (Track 34); commands Open / Draw / Close with please (Track 36); and explicit phoneme notes — the letter i has a short sound in ink and a long sound in ice-cream, with the instruction not to teach the phonemic script to the children.

**Unit 3 notes — My toys (pp. 92–115).** Letters m–t, toys, numbers 1–10 in two waves (1–5 with the song 5 Balloons, Track 45; 6–10 with the counting rhyme No More School Today, Track 49); plurals with –s "begun" in Lesson 3; How many …? counting of hidden toys; How old are you? with homemade age badges and the Happy Birthday song for Hasan, named in the notes as Ahmed and Nada's little brother, aged 4 (Track 53/54); the full telling text of the story Nada's Birthday is printed in Lesson 9.

**Unit 4 notes — Revision (pp. 116–127).** Only four lessons, completing the alphabet with u, v, w and x/y/z together; a Teaching Note states plainly that this shorter unit completes the alphabet and recycles Units 1–3. Adds receptive words glass, jug, straw (Lesson 3's party picture) and a note that uncountables can be explained with some if children ask. The telling text of In the Toy Shop is printed in Lesson 2. The unit ends with "Celebrate Units 1–4".

**Unit 5 notes — At the zoo (pp. 128–147).** The reading turn of the year: objectives add "Begin blending letter sounds to read words", and the notes demonstrate finger-blending on three-letter words (ant, car, egg, goat, horse, dog) — the only systematic decoding work in the book, always on already-known words. The song Come To The Zoo! (Track 67); adjectives big, little, long, funny with a counting rhyme (Track 70); There's … / There are … presented through the song and practised in Lesson 5; the spider story's full spoken text (Track 73) behind the pupil book's wordless version; the /sh/ sound introduced through fish with the same "do not use the phonemic script" caution; Where's the …? with in (Lesson 8) and on (Lesson 9); and, in Lesson 10, the first guided reading of full sentences (the pupil book's p. 49), read aloud in a class "reading chain".

**Unit 6 notes — My body (pp. 148–167).** Body parts and Touch your … instructions (Tracks 76–77); eye/hair colour description (My eyes are brown. My hair is black., Track 78); capital letters introduced in Lesson 1 (Poster 3) — capitals are "big", sit on the line, begin names and sentences; a sentence is explained as a group of words that makes sense, starting with a capital letter and ending with a full stop; children write their own names with an initial capital. The song Touch Your Head (Track 80); consonant-cluster blending (gr–, br–, bl–, wh–) in Lesson 4; he has / she has for monster descriptions (Lessons 5–6, Tracks 83–84); funny/scary; the monster story's telling text (Sam the clown) in Lesson 7; food revision with listening (Track 86); I wash my (face) in Lesson 9; and in Lesson 10 the first trace-and-copy of a whole sentence.

**Unit 7 notes — My family (pp. 168–185).** Family presented as Hani's family on Poster 4 (This is my dad …; his little brother Hasan and sister Amna are named in the audio, Tracks 88–90, which also drill ages: Iman 6, Hani 5, Amna 3, Hasan 4, Adel 1); the objectives add Have you any brothers/sisters? and What's his/her name?; the song My Friend (Track 91) tied to the circus story, whose telling text is printed in Lesson 3; the rhyme This Is My House (Track 93) with the house vocabulary (door, wall, window, roof, paint); true/false listening about the cutaway house (Lessons 7–8); and the number song 10 Little Elephants (Track 94) in Lesson 10, which also practises writing an alphabet sequence in capital letters.

**Unit 8 notes — Revision (pp. 186–195).** Four lessons only. Objectives are headed by assessing the year's language. Lesson 1 works the Zeinah story (Pupil Book p. 70; new character Zeinah; It's just a toy) and acting it out; Lesson 2 is listen-and-tick descriptions (Track 96) plus retelling Sami's Rocket (telling text printed); Lesson 3 is the cumulative memory game I have a pen, I have a pen and a pencil … (Track 97); Lesson 4 talks about Hani's bedroom picture, sets the year's only "mini test" (the draw-from-words Activity 1 on pupil Activities p. 159), and ends with join-the-dots races over the capital alphabet and "Celebrate Units 5–8" — teachers are told they may teach See you next school year!

**Appendices (pp. 196–213).** Transcripts: the full text of Tracks 1–97 (pp. 196–210) — every letter presentation, conversation, song and story exactly as recorded. Teacher Resource File Transcript (pp. 211–212): Tracks 98–110 — alphabet sounds and names, numbers 1–10, colours, the key vocabulary of each unit read aloud for teacher modelling, a list headed "Green text indicates language that the children will hear, but are not required to produce" (the receptive frames: What's your name?, Who's this?, Who's in the bus?, How many toys?, What is it?, How old are you?, What can you see?, How many vans can you see?, What can you see?, How many red birds?, Where's the blue tiger?), and the classroom-language track. List of picture cards (p. 213): the 120 numbered picture/word cards, colours first, then alphabetical from animals/ant to zoo.

### Grammar / language spine (as the notes present it)

The notes teach no grammar explicitly; the language is the pupil book's frames, plus a receptive layer the pupil pages never print: How are you today? — I'm fine, thank you; What's his/her name?; What's your favourite colour?; Good morning; This is my (dad); Have you any brothers/sisters? Skills spine across the notes: letter sounds and names a–z (Units 1–4) → initial-sound games → blending of known three-letter words and initial consonant clusters (Units 5–6) → sight reading of short sentences (Units 5–8); pre-writing strokes → lower-case formation → capital letters and name writing (Unit 6) → copied words → a copied sentence (Unit 6) → sentence reading-and-colouring work (Units 7–8). Plural –s is pointed out from Unit 1 Lesson 9 onward but never drilled as a rule; phonemic symbols are used in the notes for the teacher only, with repeated instructions not to teach the script to children.

### Citation-check section

### Honest negatives

- **No formal assessment scheme:** the Introduction states there is no formal assessment in the early primary grades; the whole book contains exactly one "mini test" (Unit 8, Lesson 4) and otherwise only observation charts and self-evaluation questions for the teacher. There are no answer keys beyond the answers embedded in individual procedures, no tests, no marking guidance.
- **Bilingual support stops early:** only the Introduction and Unit 1 are translated into Arabic; a teacher who needs Arabic support for Units 2–8 (the majority of the year) does not get it in this book — each later unit instead ends with a blank page.
- **The Teacher's Book adds no new pupil-facing content** beyond the pupil book's scope: no extra stories, no photocopiable masters in this volume (cards and posters belong to the separate Resource Pack), and no guidance for mixed-age or special-needs teaching beyond the Extend/Support boxes.
- **Unit-title inconsistency inside the course:** the Teacher's Book calls Unit 2 "At school" while the pupil book's Book map calls it "My school" (and the pupil book's own unit opener agrees with the Teacher's Book pattern of short titles). Also the contents page's Teaching Notes start pages for Units 2, 3 and 5 are hard to read in this copy's own contents (the listing is correct in the body: 70, 92, 128).
- Copy defects: the scan is complete and in order, including both covers; the back-cover blurb is only partly legible in this scan (white text on the dark cover reproduced almost black; its headings can be made out but not the body text). Printed pp. 91, 115, 127, 147, 167, 185 and 195 are blank by design (unit-end pages), as are PDF pp. 218–219.

## Grade 2 — English for Iraq, 2nd Primary, Pupil and Activity Book (combined pupil + activity book)

### Identity block

- **Identity verdict:** Matches the library record: this is the Grade 2 combined Pupil and Activity Book of *English for Iraq*. One completeness caveat: the Book map promises Picture/word cards at p. 185, but this file ends at p. 184 — see Honest negatives.
- **Title as printed:** *English for Iraq* — "2nd Primary" — "Pupil and Activity Book" (front cover, PDF p. 1; the book has no separate title page — the imprint follows the cover directly).
- **Authors as printed:** "Jenny Quintana and Helen O'Neill with Sarah MacBurnie" (cover). The cover also carries the Editorial and Adaptation Committee box (Ministry of Education English Curriculum Committee).
- **Publisher:** Garnet Publishing Ltd (Garnet Education), 8 Southern Court, South Street, Reading RG1 4QS, UK (imprint, p. 2).
- **Edition / year as printed:** "First edition, copyright © 2014 Garnet Publishing Ltd." Production code: IRQ14 PB 2ndP.
- **ISBN as printed:** No ISBN printed on the imprint page, and this PDF has no back cover where one might appear.
- **PDF pages:** 184.
- **Printed vs PDF offset:** None — printed page = PDF page throughout (cover = p. 1, imprint + Icon Key = p. 2, Book map = p. 3, last page = p. 184). All page references below are printed pages.
- **Text-layer character:** Effectively empty (184 characters total, nothing on any page). Read whole from 4-up contact sheets of page renders.
- **Cast / design:** Full colour pupil section; black-and-white Activities. A wholly new cast replaces the Grade 1 children: **Zack**, a small green alien who arrives in Unit 1 and leaves Earth in Unit 8; children **Hadi**, **Deena** and **Kareem** (Deena's brother Hadi is 10, her brother Kareem is 7, per the Activities model text); their mum and dad; and Sam the clown from Grade 1, who is the cover figure and the Activities mascot. Six main units of eight lessons each, two revision units of four lessons; every main unit ends with a picture story in Lesson 8. The pupil section's writing is limited to single words and numbers; all sentence writing lives in the Activities.

### Unit-by-unit summary

**Unit 1 (pp. 4–13).** A restart-and-extend unit. Lesson 1 opens with Zack meeting the children (I'm Hadi. / Hello, I'm Deena. / I'm Zack. / I'm Kareem.) under the question What's your name?, then extends Grade 1's frames: What's his/her name? with bare-name answers, How old are you? — I'm 7 / 10. Lesson 2, Toy shop, practises What's this? — It's a book and counting (How many watches? — Five). Lesson 3 is the song Five little ducks (full lyrics in the Songs section). Lesson 4, My robot, revises body words (head, nose, eye, ear, hand, mouth, body, foot, leg) through description (This is my robot. Her body is yellow…). Lesson 5 teaches How are you? properly for the first time — answers I'm very well, thanks. / I'm fine. / I'm not very well! — with the action song If you're happy…. Lesson 6, The park, is a big counting picture (How many trees/bags are there?). Lesson 7 practises There are … statements with Yes!/No! checks. Lesson 8 is the story **The toy maker** (pp. 12–13): a Pinocchio-like tale told in 12 captioned frames with a few speech bubbles (A new toy!, A toy camel!, I'm a toy clown!, New dresses.) and a read-and-find task.

**Unit 2 (pp. 14–23).** Food and likes. Fruit set: banana, pear, mango, grapes, plum, watermelon, dates, lemon, apple, orange (plus carrot in the map). I like pears. / I don't like bananas. from Lesson 1; The fruit song (Lesson 2: "What's your favourite fruit?"); the café set (soup, sandwich, pizza, milk, tea, water, juice, cake, ice-cream) from a menu picture (Lesson 3), where third-person likes appear (He likes soup. / She likes sandwiches.); Lesson 4 is explicitly about **a / an** (It's a lemon. / It's an orange.), with listen-and-tick article choices; Lessons 5–6 teach the offer exchange Do you want an orange? — Yes, please. / No, thank you., including a first short reading sentence in the lunchbox picture (In my lunchbox there is a sandwich, an apple and a banana, a cake and some water. Yummy! — the book's only some). Lesson 7 is a café role-play grid (I want a/an …). Lesson 8 is the story **The big carrot** (the enormous-turnip folk tale retold with a carrot; Pull!, It's out!, and a final frame labelled Carrot Soup).

**Unit 3 (pp. 24–33).** House and position. Lesson 1, My bedroom, is a read-and-find over a labelled room (wardrobe, shelf, lamp, picture, bed, bin, CD player, desk, chair, trainers, skates), with Where's the car? — On the mat. Lesson 2 (Where's the monkey?) presents in / on / under with a monkey and a hat, then full read-and-match sentences (The balls are under the hat.). Lesson 3 tours the house (bedroom, dining room, sitting room, bathroom, kitchen, garden) with Where's Deena/Hadi/Kareem? — In the (room). Lesson 4 adds next to and between (What's in your bedroom?). Lesson 5 is the action song This is the way we…. Lesson 6 adds What can you see? — I can see a bag. Lesson 7 teaches I'm hungry. / I'm thirsty. with the polite sequence Do you want a sandwich? / some juice? — Yes, please. — Here you are. — Thank you. Lesson 8 is **Goldilocks and the three bears** (pp. 32–33), with printed story speech (Where's my house?, There's a house!, This soup is just right., This bed is just right., My chair!, My soup!, There's somebody in my bed!).

**Unit 4 — Revision (pp. 34–37).** Four lessons: spot-the-difference bedrooms; a zoo listening ("Who am I?"); the hungry/thirsty dialogue re-set as a listening-order task; and a mini picture story, **Hungry Muna Mouse**.

**Unit 5 (pp. 38–47).** Clothes and numbers to 20. The clothes set (hat, T-shirt, trousers, skirt, shirt, dress, trainers, socks, shoes, skates) is presented on a washing line, with I'm wearing … from the first lesson (I'm wearing a red T-shirt. / I'm wearing blue trousers.) — used as a set phrase, never analysed as a tense. Numbers 11–15 come with counting in a shoe shop (Lesson 2: How many trainers/socks/shoes are there?) and 16–20 in Lesson 4, which also teaches It's long. / They're short. for clothes. Lesson 3 adds big / small / long / short with the song Kareem put your trainers on. Lesson 5 is description listening (She's wearing a black skirt and a blue shirt. She has a bag.) over a line of named people. Lesson 6 presents has for possessions (Hadi has a ball. / Zack has a watch. / Kareem has a car. / Deena has a kite.) beside I have …. Lesson 7 builds the personal introduction: My name's Sami. / I have a sister. Her name is Safa. / I'm 11 years old. / I have a brother. His name is Kareem. He's 7. Lesson 8 is **Layla and the wolf** (Little Red Riding Hood with an Iraqi name; Hello, Grandma., Your eyes/ears/teeth are big!, It's a wolf! — the wolf is tied up by the woodcutter, not killed).

**Unit 6 (pp. 48–57).** Family, places and days. Lesson 1 is a birthday-party scene naming mum, dad, uncle, aunt, brother, cousin, sister, grandma, grandpa, with Who is this? — It's her grandpa. / It's my/her/his …. Lesson 2, Tom's family, adds describing adjectives for people: tall, short, old, young, small (Tom's sister is small and she is young. / His dad is tall. / My mum is short.). Lesson 3 teaches places with at: Where's Kareem? — He's at home. / Where are dad and grandpa? — They're at the café. (at home, at the café, at the market, at the fun park, at school). Lesson 4 is the days of the week (Sunday first) with the printed **7 days song** ("I like Fridays. How about you?"). Lesson 5 practises sentence matching (We're … / I'm … / It's Saturday. / My cake has 10 candles.). Lesson 6 is a numbers game to 20. Lesson 7, Deena's party, recycles has and positions (He has a red hat. / He's under the chair.). Lesson 8, **My week**, is the year's most text-like story: seven captioned day panels in the first person plural (On Sunday, we visit family. On Monday, we clean the house. On Tuesday, we go shopping. On Wednesday, we cook. On Thursday, we go to the park. On Friday, we have a big lunch. On Saturday, we have a picnic.).

**Unit 7 (pp. 58–67).** Ability and routines. The fair set (swing, slide, roundabout, seesaw, big wheel) frames can: Lesson 1 presents action verbs (draw, ride a bike, play football, swim, sing, play basketball) through Can you draw? — Yes, I can. / Can you ride a bike? — No, I can't. Lesson 2 adds I can jump. / I can play the drum. / I can run fast. / I can't play football. Lesson 3 builds routine sentences with places and days (I watch TV in my bedroom. / I do my homework in my bedroom. / I cook in the kitchen. / I ride my bike in the park. / I sleep in my bedroom.; sentence building I + verb + on + day). Lesson 4 spells names aloud (What's her name? — Her name is Nada. N-a-d-a.). Lessons 5–6 teach feelings with the fair and a lost-ball mini-story: happy, sad; How do you feel?; How does Kareem feel?; I'm happy / sad / hungry / thirsty / tired. Lesson 8 is **The rabbit and the tortoise** (I can run fast and I can jump. / I can walk. / I'm slow. / I'm tired.).

**Unit 8 — Revision (pp. 68–71).** Four lessons: food likes revision; Sami's friends (a short printed description text: He has long hair and brown eyes. He is tall. He is sad. He can play football.); an animal-guessing game built on can (It can't jump. It has 8 legs. / It can fly and it can sing. / It likes bananas!); and Zack's goodbye, a printed farewell song (Zack says Goodbye) as Zack leaves in his spaceship.

**Picture dictionary (pp. 72–75).** About ninety picture words in alphabetical order (banana … wolf), each with its first letter printed in red — the book's only nod to initial sounds in the pupil section.

**Songs and rhymes (pp. 76–80).** Full printed lyrics for the year's songs: Five little ducks; If you're happy…; The fruit song; This is the way we…; Kareem put your trainers on (first line printed "Kareem put your sandals on", then "trainers" — as printed); the 7 days song; I'm happy…; Zack's goodbye.

**Activities (pp. 81–184, black-and-white, two pages per lesson).** A workbook with its own two long strands running beside the unit content. (1) A handwriting and spelling strand: Units 1–2 work through the **lower-case alphabet a–z** one or two letters per lesson (trace and copy with starting dots, initial-letter completion of Grade 1 key words — _pple, _gg, _lephant, _oat, _uice — letter counting, anagrams, word searches, syllable matching), plus tracing the numerals 1–10; Units 5–6 then work through the **capital letters A–Z** the same way, with upper/lower matching, "write the small letters", and case-correction exercises (haDi → Hadi, muM, Egg, deena; later daD, Table, baLloon, fridAy). (2) A content strand mirroring each pupil lesson: matching, counting, tick/cross reading of full sentences (There is a desk in the room.), gap-fill dialogues (Do you want … apple?), a like/likes choice exercise (I __ soup. / He __ sandwiches. / She __ tea.), a/an writing, preposition completions, vowel completion (a, e, i, o or u), reading-and-drawing from short texts (a five-sentence house description; a mini letter "Hello Nada — I have a new watch…"), the only guided personal writing in the book ("Write about you", p. 149, copying the model "My name is Kareem. I am 7. I have 1 sister…"), simple addition sums presented in English (9 + 7 =, What's 10+2?), sentence unscrambling (school I go on Monday to → I go to school on Monday), and, in Unit 8, a full crossword with written Across/Down clues and a frame-written postcard to Zack (Hello Zack, My name is …. I am …. I like …. I don't like …. I like to …. Goodbye!) before the final page: "It's the end! How do you feel?" and "Goodbye! Well done!" (p. 184).

### Grammar spine

be in all persons (I'm, he's/she's, it's, we're, they're) with questions What's this?, What's his/her name?, Who is this?, Where's …? / Where are …?; plurals and There is/are … with How many … are there?; likes with third-person –s shown but not explained (I like / I don't like; He/She likes); the articles **a / an** taught explicitly as a choice (Unit 2) and some appearing once in a reading sentence; offers with Do you want …?; prepositions in, on, under, next to, between and at (places); possessives my / your / his / her and name + 's in titles and single sentences (Tom's family, Kareem's sister) without explanation; has vs have for possession (I have … / Deena has … / He has …); present continuous only as the fixed chunk I'm wearing / He's wearing for clothes; present simple routines (I watch TV / I do my homework / On Sunday, we visit family); can / can't for ability with Can you …? — Yes, I can. / No, I can't.; days of the week with on; numbers to 20. All of it is presented as phrases and exchanges — there is not one grammar rule, table or explanation anywhere in the book.

### Honest negatives

- **Incomplete copy:** the Book map (p. 3) lists "Picture/word cards — page 185", but this PDF ends at p. 184 with the Activities farewell page; there are no card pages and no back cover in the file. (In the printed book the cards presumably follow; they are simply absent here.) Because there is no back cover, no ISBN can be checked — none is printed on the imprint page either.
- **No phonics programme:** the Grade 1 decoding strand is not continued — no blending work, no new letter-sound teaching in the pupil lessons; letter work is confined to handwriting and initial-letter spelling in the Activities, and the picture dictionary's red first letters. Reading is whole-word and sentence-pattern based.
- **Almost no connected prose:** the longest texts are the seven one-sentence day panels of My week, a five-sentence house description and a six-sentence personal model in the Activities; everything else is captions, exchanges and lists. There are no comprehension questions on any text.
- **No past tense, no future forms, no comparatives, no classroom-instruction language teaching, no numbers above 20** (party balloons in Unit 6 are numbered to 30 as decoration, but numbers are taught only to 20), and no Arabic anywhere.
- Copy/print notes: the songs page prints "Kareem put your sandals on" as the first line of a song whose title and remaining lines say "trainers" (p. 79); the Unit 3 Lesson 5 "Read and write" exercise on p. 122 numbers its items 1, 2, 4, 5 — item 3 is missing as printed; an Activities tick/cross item on p. 136 prints the ungrammatical "Deena is wearing skirt." as one of the statements to judge. This scanned copy also carries a pupil's faint pencil completions on a number of Activities pages. The scan itself is complete and in order from front cover to p. 184.

## Grade 2 — English for Iraq, 2nd Primary, Teacher's Book (teacher's guide)

### Identity block

- **Identity verdict:** The file is the genuine Grade 2 teacher's book for *English for Iraq*, wrapped in a third-party scanner's cover page and page stamps (see below). As with Grade 1, the book's own printed kind is **"Teacher's Book"**, not "Teacher's Guide" as the library record has it.
- **Title as printed:** *English for Iraq* — "2nd Primary" — "Teacher's Book" (the book's own front cover, PDF p. 2, and title page, PDF p. 3).
- **Authors as printed:** Jenny Quintana and Helen O'Neill with Sarah MacBurnie (cover and title page). The title page also credits the Editorial and Adaptation Committee (Ministry of Education English Curriculum Committee; Malek Mohamed Juda, Head of the English Curriculum; Adil Ali Moussa; Muayyad Naji Ahmed; Ramzi Nouri Abdullah).
- **Publisher:** Garnet Publishing Ltd (Garnet Education), 8 Southern Court, South Street, Reading RG1 4QS, UK (imprint, PDF p. 4).
- **Edition / year as printed:** "First edition, copyright © 2014 Garnet Publishing Ltd." Production code: IRQ14 TB 2ndP.
- **ISBN as printed:** No ISBN printed anywhere; the back cover carries only a stock/library sticker ("TKG003097").
- **PDF pages:** 203.
- **Printed vs PDF offset:** Printed page = PDF page − 4, counting only the book's own pages. PDF p. 1 is **not part of the book** — it is a scanner wrapper page (below). PDF p. 2 = the book's front cover, PDF p. 3 = title page, PDF p. 4 = imprint, PDF p. 5 = printed p. 1 (Contents), printed pp. 2–198 follow in order (last numbered page: 198, a ruled Notes page, PDF p. 202), and PDF p. 203 is the back cover. All page references below are printed pages.
- **Scanner wrapper (not part of the book):** PDF p. 1 is a cover sheet added by the "Education for Iraq" website (موقع التعليم لأجل العراق), crediting the scan to **Hussein Kareem** and describing the file in Arabic as the complete English teacher's guide for 2nd Primary, with a dedication of the work to the martyrs of Iraq and specifically to the scanner's brother. Every scanned page also carries the site's purple Arabic header stamp and a footer line claiming the site's copyright in the scan, and the book's title page bears the site's large circular watermark. Two book pages (pp. 87 and 115, both unit-boundary pages) are given over entirely to the site's full-page circular logo (an Iraq-map emblem); whatever stood on those pages in the printed book — at most a divider or blank — cannot be recovered from this file. Page 13 of the Introduction is scanned upside down.
- **Text-layer character:** The PDF's only text layer is the scanner site's repeated Arabic header/footer stamp on every page (~100 characters per page); the book itself is image-only. Read whole from 4-up contact sheets of page renders.
- **Design / language:** Black-and-white. Like the Grade 1 book, the Introduction (pp. 3–25) and the whole of Unit 1's notes (pp. 28–69) are **fully bilingual** — each English page is followed by its Arabic translation — and Units 2–8 are English-only. Course components: Pupil and Activity Book, Teacher's Book, Audio CD (105 lesson tracks), Teacher Resource File audio (Tracks 106–115), Picture/Word Cards (80 listed), pupil's mini picture/word cards, and five posters (Poster 1: My body; 2: My house; 3: Numbers and Days of the Week; 4: Birthday party; 5: My day).

### Section-by-section summary

**Contents (p. 1).** Introduction (pp. 3–25, including Key features, Components, The course, Aims, Advice to teachers, Teaching the course, Book map pp. 26–27); Teaching Notes Units 1–8 (pp. 29–179); Appendices: Transcripts (pp. 180–194), List of Picture/Word Cards (p. 195), Teacher Resource File Transcript (pp. 196–197), Notes (p. 198).

**Introduction (pp. 3–25).** Largely the Grade 1 introduction re-aimed at seven-year-olds. Key features stress "revision of initial sound recognition and sound-letter correlation, as introduced in 1st Primary", guided writing and tracing, and the picture dictionary carried over from Grade 1. The course is eight units and **56 lessons**; Units 4 and 8 are four-lesson revision units, and Lesson 8 of every other unit is a story. The characters are listed as Hadi, Deena and Kareem (brother and sisters), Deena's friend Nada, the parents and family, and Zack, "their alien friend from another planet". The aims are stated to be **the same eight aims as 1st Primary** (including phonics-and-whole-word reading, and handwriting extended to "words and simple sentences"). "Teaching the course" (pp. 13–25) is the methodological core: six listening text types; **continuous assessment** (the teacher keeps a written record of each pupil's achievements in reading and speaking — there is still no test anywhere in the course); homework policy (stars rather than criticism: a star for completion, two for good work, three for excellent work); speaking types and procedures; and a full reading section naming the course's three reading approaches — **whole-word (and phrase) recognition, a phonic approach, and audio-assisted reading** — with sentence-building from Word Cards used to "focus attention on grammatical features such as word order". Writing is split into the mechanics of writing (the Activities handwriting strand: revision of lower-case, then capital letters, then words, short sentences and punctuation) and activity writing. Songs are flagged as containing "grammatical features that the pupils have not yet studied, such as the past tense", which need not be explained.

**Book map (pp. 26–27).** Per-unit Aims and Language columns for all eight units — the book's own one-page-per-half-year syllabus, tracking the pupil book's map but adding teacher-level items (e.g. Unit 1: "Do you have any (brothers/sisters)?"; Unit 5: have/has and "My name's … / I am (10). (He's/She's) (9). I have (a brother/a sister)").

**Teaching notes — pattern (pp. 29–179).** Each unit opens with Objectives in the four skills, then a Language / Word list by lesson (in Unit 1 an asterisk marks language revised from 1st Primary, with the printed note that in Unit 1 "much of the language is revised"; from Unit 2 a teaching note says only new vocabulary is listed). Each lesson then has an Aims / Language / Materials box and staged procedures (Warm up, numbered Stages, Homework, Rounding off, Extend/Support boxes), with all audio transcripts printed inline and answers supplied where an activity has a fixed answer (e.g. the eleven spot-the-difference answers in Unit 4, Lesson 1). Arabic is used inside the English notes too, as a teaching tool: story preparation, concept checks and game explanations are repeatedly scripted as "In Arabic, tell the pupils…".

**Unit 1 notes (pp. 29–69, bilingual).** The revision unit taught as new-course induction: the teacher is told to discuss the Grade 1 course in Arabic, introduce Zack as an alien from outer space, and re-teach the greetings with ages (the transcripts fix the cast: Hadi is 10, Deena is 9, Kareem is 7). New-in-print language beyond the pupil pages: Do you have any brothers/sisters? and How many?, taught in Lesson 3. Letters a–m are re-taught for sound and formation, one or two per lesson, with the "/a/ for ant" convention and air-writing. The story lesson prints the full text of **The toy maker** (Track 11): a past-tense narrative about Joha the toy maker and his wife Mariam, whose wood is turned into toys overnight by fairies — the teacher's preparation and most picture questions are scripted in Arabic, and the story's own questions are in the past tense (Where did Joha go? What did he buy?), though pupils answer with single words.

**Unit 2 notes (pp. 70–87).** Letters n–z complete the lower-case revision (the alphabet caterpillar on Activities p. 113 marks the end). The objectives contain the book's only grammar-awareness objective: "Develop awareness of –s with verbs in third person singular", and Lesson 3 carries its only explicit grammar teaching note: if a food is countable "we add an s on the end", if uncountable we don't — She likes sandwiches (with the –s "pronounced /ɪz/") but He likes water. The a/an lesson (Lesson 4) is deliberately practical: "Do not go into detail about vowels and consonants here", but the vowels are written on the board and pupils practise an apple, an egg, an ice-cream, an orange, an umbrella because "it is easier to say 'an apple' than it is to say 'a apple'". The story text of **The big carrot** (Tracks 23–24) is present-tense narration naming Ibrahim, his wife Dalal, little Zeinah and her brother Mahmoud; p. 87 is the scanner-logo page.

**Unit 3 notes (pp. 88–105).** No writing strand in this unit beyond word completion (the capital-letter strand has not yet begun; handwriting objectives are word-level). Lesson 7's note explains some for the teacher: "we use some with liquids like juice, milk and water because we can't count them, and that we use a/an with things we can count, like a sandwich" — with permission to teach it simply as some juice, some milk, some water "without direct reference as to why". The full **Goldilocks** text (Tracks 40–43) is printed: present-tense narration whose direct speech contains the book's only present-perfect lines (Who's been sitting in my chair? / Who's been eating my soup? / Who's been sleeping in my bed?), never commented on.

**Unit 4 notes — Revision (pp. 106–115).** Four lessons: spot-the-difference with all eleven differences listed; the animal-description listening (Track 46: five animals describing themselves — Quack the duck, Mimi the elephant, Penny the penguin, Fred the fish, Suzi the monkey); the hungry/thirsty dialogue as story-ordering; and **Hungry Muna Mouse** (Tracks 49–50), whose full present-tense text is printed, including the muddled-order version used for the ordering task. P. 115 is the second scanner-logo page.

**Unit 5 notes (pp. 116–133).** The capital-letter strand begins: two capitals per lesson (A–N across the unit) with the same "big, sit on the line, same sound" explanation as Grade 1, plus the definition of a sentence as "a group of words that make sense" starting with a capital letter and ending with a full stop. Notes add: numbers taught in two waves (11–15, then 16–20) with spoken addition ("What's seven and two?"); the instruction to point out "the difference between It's/They're" for clothes; a lesson note that pupils describing typical Iraqi clothes "can use the Arabic words"; and the has-lesson procedure built on a chain game (Mohammed has a key…). The full **Layla and the wolf** text (Tracks 62–64) is printed — present-tense narration with present-continuous colour (Layla is walking in the forest. She is going to see her grandma.) and the wolf's "I want to eat you"; the woodcutter (here "the man from the forest", named Hamid in a later note) ties the wolf up.

**Unit 6 notes (pp. 134–153).** Capitals O–Z complete the alphabet of capitals. Notes add: the teacher may let pupils join in numbers 21–30 from the party balloons "but it is not necessary for pupils really to learn 21–30 at this stage"; at home / at school are to be learned idiomatically ("it is not necessary at this stage to teach why home and school do not need 'the'"); the days are taught with alliteration prompts and the pronunciation note that Wednesday is /ˈwenzdeɪ/ and that th "makes the sound /θ/" in Thursday; and a named technique, "Recognizing word groups: vocabulary building" (a spider-diagram grouping of words round a theme). The **My week** audio text (Track 83) is fuller than the pupil book's captions (it adds seeing grandma and grandpa, helping mum, liking cooking, the brother playing football, and the picnic food). P. 153 is blank.

**Unit 7 notes (pp. 154–171).** The can/can't unit: a pronunciation note distinguishes the a of can (short /æ/) from the a of can't (the "ar" sound /ɑː/, "as in 'father'"). Lesson 3's listening requires the only other explicit grammar conversion in the book: pupils "will have to change I to He/She and add –s (–es for 'do') to the verb" (He watches in his bedroom). Name-spelling is taught with How do you spell …? (K-a-r-e-e-m), and the feelings lessons add How does (Kareem) feel? The full **Rabbit and the tortoise** text (Track 100) is printed — a past-tense narration (said, laughed, thought, came, blew, shouted, ran, jumped, fell asleep, kept on walking, woke up, "The tortoise has won the race!") — with the moral discussed in Arabic. P. 171 is blank.

**Unit 8 notes — Revision (pp. 172–179).** Four lessons: the countable/uncountable sort made explicit as two board columns, "Without s" (fish, milk, ice-cream, water, tea, juice, cake, watermelon, pizza) and "With –s" (dates, grapes, apples, pears, bananas, mangoes), with pupils reminded "to put an –s at the end of the countable things"; the crossword with its answers (Track 102); "word-by-word reading" of the Sami's friends paragraph (25 words, counted in the note) round the class; the animal guessing game; and the goodbye letter read aloud pupil by pupil, ending with the book's own list of what the children have learned and "See you next school year!".

**Appendices (pp. 180–198).** Transcripts: Tracks 1–105 in full (pp. 180–194). List of Picture/Word Cards (p. 195): 80 cards, banana to wolf. Teacher Resource File Transcript (pp. 196–197): Track 106 (the word list read aloud), Tracks 107–114 (the key language of each unit, spoken as a teacher model — including, for Unit 1, the receptive frames and "Do you have any [brothers/sisters]?"), and Track 115, "Useful classroom language" (praise, organisation, instructions, endings). A ruled Notes page (p. 198) and the back cover close the book.

### Grammar / language spine (as the notes present it)

The pupil book's frames, plus a small receptive/teacher-led layer: Do you have any brothers/sisters?; How is he/she?; past-tense story comprehension questions (Where did Joha go?); How do you spell …?; How does (he) feel? The only points at which the notes explain language rather than drill it are four: third-person –s on verbs (countable food takes –s: She likes sandwiches, /ɪz/); a/an practised through the vowel letters without a stated rule; some with uncountables, optionally unexplained; the countable/uncountable –s sort in Unit 8; and the I → he/she + –s conversion in one Unit 7 listening. Past tense and the present perfect occur only inside story audio (The toy maker, The rabbit and the tortoise; Goldilocks' Who's been …?), which the Introduction expressly says need not be explained. Sentence-level literacy is defined and taught in Unit 5 (capital letter, full stop, "a group of words that make sense").

### Honest negatives

- **Wrapper contamination:** the file is not a clean publisher scan. PDF p. 1 is the scanning website's own cover/dedication page, every page is stamped with the site's Arabic header/footer, the title page carries its large circular watermark, and printed pp. 87 and 115 are wholly replaced by the site's full-page logo. The genuine book underneath is complete and in order (contents, all eight units, all appendices, back cover), but anything printed on pp. 87 and 115 — most likely blank or divider pages at unit boundaries — is lost in this copy. Printed p. 13 is scanned upside down, p. 75 is noticeably blurred, and the p. 82 footer misprints the series title as "Enalish for Iraq Teacher's Book" (as printed).
- **Bilingual support again stops after Unit 1:** the Introduction and Unit 1 are fully translated into Arabic; Units 2–8 (pp. 70–179, the bulk of the teaching year) are English-only, although the English notes themselves repeatedly direct the teacher to explain and discuss in Arabic.
- **No assessment instruments:** assessment remains the Introduction's continuous-observation record and homework stars; there are no tests, no answer keys as such (answers are embedded in procedures), and no photocopiable material in this volume.
- The Teacher's Book adds no pupil-facing texts beyond the pupil book's own: its unique content is the full audio story texts, the transcripts (Tracks 1–115), the card list, and the method guidance summarised above.

## Grade 4 — English for Iraq 4th Primary Pupil's Book (pupil's book)

### Identity block

- **Identity verdict vs the record title:** MATCH in substance, with the series' own naming. The record calls this book "English for Iraq — Student's Book"; the book itself is titled **Pupil's Book** on its cover (the record's "Student's Book" is the manifest's generic label for the pupil book at every grade).
- **Title as printed:** *English for Iraq — 4th Primary — Pupil's Book* (front cover, PDF p. 1).
- **Authors as printed:** Sarah MacBurnie (cover and imprint; the imprint asserts her right as author). The imprint (PDF p. 2) also thanks Faten Fakhri Khalaf (Primary Education Specialist) and Hala Talib Hammood (Trainer and Co-ordinator to the English Curriculum Committee) for support to the Editorial and Adaptation Committee.
- **Publisher:** Garnet Publishing Ltd., 8 Southern Court, South Street, Reading RG1 4QS, UK (imprint). Cover carries "Garnet EDUCATION" and the Republic of Iraq, Ministry of Education emblem. Photography credits (123rf, Alamy, Dreamstime, Getty, iStockphoto, Shutterstock) and two photo acknowledgements printed on the imprint page.
- **Edition/year as printed:** "First edition © 2016 Garnet Publishing Ltd." (imprint, PDF p. 2). Production code printed on the imprint: IRQ16 PB 4thP.
- **ISBN as printed:** No ISBN printed — none on the cover, imprint or last page of this copy.
- **PDF pages:** 119, all read.
- **Printed vs PDF offset:** Printed page = PDF page (offset 0) through the whole lesson body — the printed folio on Unit 1 Lesson 1 is 6 at PDF p. 6, and folios track the PDF pagination one-for-one to the end of the units (printed p. 109 = PDF p. 109). The closing "Your Dictionary" (printed pp. 110–120) is complete in the file, but its last spreads are scanned with facing pages out of order (folio 119 appears before 118, 117 before 116) and the final dictionary page, printed p. 120 (Unit Seven wordlist), sits at PDF p. 119 — the printed book runs to 120 pages while the PDF holds 119 leaves. All page references below are to the printed pages.
- **Text-layer character:** None — image-only scan (pdftotext returns only form feeds). Read whole from 100-DPI 4-up contact sheets, with a RapidOCR pass over the page renders as a search index. Clean colour scan, no scanner wrapper pages.
- **Cast/design:** Full-colour illustrated course book, 8 units of 8 lessons each (Units 4 and 8 are 4-lesson revision units). Recurring cast: Yousif (9), his sister Lina (8), Nadia (9) and Kareem (9), at Green Tree School, with Tariq, Rana, Khaled, Adel, Suzan, Muna and Rami in supporting roles. Every unit ends with a picture story in Lesson 8; Units 3 and 7 carry a "P for Project!" spread. A Book Map (printed pp. 3–5) tabulates each unit's vocabulary and core language, and an English–Arabic "Your Dictionary" (pp. 110–120) closes the book.

### Unit-by-unit summary

**Unit 1 (pp. 6–21) — New friends, playground and school things.** The cast introduces itself with ages, family and favourites. Playground vocabulary (tunnel, swing, slide, ladder, roundabout, bars) with the present continuous for actions now (*I'm climbing up the ladder. I'm crawling through the tunnel.*) and the movement prepositions up, down, through, along, around. Playground rules give negative imperatives (*Do not stand on the swing. Don't go too fast!*), with a "Let's check" box teaching *do not = don't*. "What's in my school bag?" introduces *there is a … / there are some …*. Comparatives arrive in Lesson 5 ("What's the difference?"): opposite adjectives (big/small, long/short, old/young, tall/short) and -er forms (*I am taller than my brother; This ruler is longer*), practised in the rhyme "Bigger and smaller". Lesson 6 reads two short emails about tomorrow's lessons and teaches the sequencers *first, then, after that* with the school timetable (*What's on Tuesday? — First, it's maths. Then, it's English.*). Lesson 7, "The school shop", teaches *Whose … is this? — It's Lina's* (possessive 's) and top/middle/bottom for shelves. Lesson 8 is the story *The Lion and the Mouse*, narrated in the past simple.

**Unit 2 (pp. 22–37) — Around the world and my country.** Countries (Iraq, England, America, Russia, China, Brazil) on a world map with *I'm from Iraq. Where are you from?* and a "Let's check" on capital letters for names and countries. Information files compare Iraq and England (capital city, language, weather, population) with comparatives (*Summer in Iraq is hotter*). The "Big Numbers" rhyme teaches 100–1000 (*We say one thousand*). "A school day" (Adel's text) and "My weekend" (Liz's text) practise the present simple, times of day and school subjects; Lesson 5, "More time", teaches telling the time (3 o'clock, 3.30) and *Can you help me? — Yes, I'll try*. Lesson 6, "Geography — my country", teaches landscape words (mountain, desert, stream, river, village, lake, field, city, oil field, north/south) with a reading text about Iraq — its mountains, desert, the Tigris and Euphrates, oil and date palms. Lesson 7 is a describing-and-guessing game (*It has one leg and it moves its arms in the wind*). Lesson 8 is the story *City Mouse and Village Mouse*.

**Unit 3 (pp. 38–53) — School Open Day.** A school open day in May frames the unit: stalls, races and games. Drinks (coffee, tea, lemonade, cola, orange juice, mango juice, water, milk) and snacks (cakes, watermelon, cookies, sandwiches, pizza, sweets) are bought and sold: *Can I help you? Can I have a cola, please? What do you want? — I want a cup of tea. How much are the cakes? — They're 250 IQD each.* Months of the year are taught in a rhyme with *When is your birthday? — It's in March*. Clothes for the races (T-shirt, trainers, trousers) extend the possessive (*This is Lina's T-shirt. Yousif's trainers are too big*). Two emails (Lina's and Suzan's) recount the day in the past simple (*I ran in the egg and spoon race. I was first!*). Ordinal numbers first (1st) to fourth (4th) are taught in "Numbers in order" and used for race results (*Who was first in the race? Tariq was first*). Lesson 7 is the project: make an Open Day poster. Lesson 8 is the story *Sports Races*, told in the past with the adverbs quickly, slowly, loudly, carefully.

**Unit 4 (pp. 54–57) — Revision of Units 1–3.** Four lessons only: a two-team class challenge answering questions from Units 1–3; "My progress" (completing sentences, the shop dialogue *Can I help you? — Can I have 2, please?*, months questions); the choose-your-own-words story *The Magic Bird*, which pupils complete with adjectives and comparatives and then retell; and a Snakes and ladders language game.

**Unit 5 (pp. 58–73) — A school trip.** Places to see (museum, lake, cave, forest, waterfall, ruin, seaside, river, mountain range, bridge) with describing adjectives (ancient, beautiful, large, interesting, famous, high, dangerous, favourite) and an information file on the Iraqi Museum (its lion's head, gold bracelets, ancient writing and a guffa boat from the Tigris). A class vote with bar charts asks *How many pupils want to see the seaside?*. *Going to* for plans runs through the trip lessons (*We're going to see a waterfall. I'm going to take my backpack*), alongside the present continuous for what people on the train are doing now (*Yousif is drawing a picture. Tariq is sleeping*) and trip rules in imperatives (*Sit quietly. Do not talk loudly*). Numbers extend to 1000, 2000, 3000; prices in the Waterfall Shop reach 3000 IQD. Lesson 5 reads about the mountains of Iraq (the Zagros range; Cheeka Dar, 3,611 metres, the highest mountain; Halgurd second highest) and sets a mini-project on Iraq's rivers with deliberately wrong words to spot and correct. Lesson 6, "Higher and highest", teaches superlatives (*A is high. B is higher. C is the highest; The Euphrates is the longest river in Iraq; Which is the biggest — a car, a truck or a train?*). Lesson 8 is the story *Rami and the School Trip*.

**Unit 6 (pp. 74–89) — Yesterday: questions and the past.** The wh- question words are systematised (who, what, when, where, how, why — *why* glossed as "means because"). "Yesterday" sets yesterday/today/tomorrow against the days of the week with instruction sequencers (First … Then … Next … After that … Finally …). Two short texts about Muna's day give the first explicit past-simple work: pupils match present and past sentences and extract the verb pairs (go/went, is/was, play/played, do/did). The "Question monster game" builds silly questions from when/who/how + answer banks including the -ly adverbs. "Nadia's grandma" and "Nadia's grandpa" use *was/were* and past verbs for childhood memories (*When I was 9, I was tall. I had long, black hair. I played skipping at school. I always did my homework*). Lesson 5, "At the cinema", is built on Yousif's email about a robot film, answered with did-questions (*Where did he go? He went to the cinema. What did he see? Who did he go with?*); Lesson 6 is the rhyme "Where did you go?" (*I went to the zoo. I saw elephants, and monkeys, too*). Lesson 7 teaches *There was a book / There were books* in a memory game. Lesson 8 is the story *The Little Brown Bird*.

**Unit 7 (pp. 90–105) — The school challenge.** An invitation email from Red River School opens a four-challenge contest (sports, English, maths, art). Pupils learn to say what people need (*What do I need for maths? — You need a ruler and a pencil*) and the has/needs contrast (*he has … / he needs …*). The rhyme "Have you got …?" introduces *have got* with *some/any* (*Have you got a ruler? — Yes, I have. / No, I haven't, but I need some crayons. Can I borrow yours?*). Lesson 4 teaches must/mustn't for rules (*I must clean my teeth. I mustn't talk in a test. You must put on your badges. Remember! You mustn't talk*), followed by the project: a must/mustn't rules poster. Lesson 5 works with the challenge form (name, age, school, challenge, "I need: …"). Lesson 6, "Maths and art", teaches the shapes square, rectangle, triangle with ruler-measuring tasks. Lesson 7 scores the English challenge (matching and spelling) and the sports challenge (ball-and-bucket race). Lesson 8 is the story *The Big Bike Race*, which recycles *has got/hasn't got* and *must/mustn't*.

**Unit 8 (pp. 106–109) — Revision of Units 5–7.** Four lessons: "Test your partner" (paired quiz across Units 5–7: adverbs, word order in questions, *going to*, past verbs, *have got*, must/mustn't); "My progress" (place-word definitions and sorting answer words under How/Who/When/Where); the story *Ice-creams for Everyone!*, a cloze story completed with past-tense verbs (looked, had, was, helped, saw); and a pronunciation board game asking what sounds 'ch', 'sh', 'ph', 'gh', 'ee' and the -ly ending make — the book's only explicit phonics work.

**Your Dictionary (pp. 110–120).** An English–Arabic wordlist organised by unit (Units One to Seven; the revision Unit 8 has no list, and the revision Unit 4 contributes only three words: date palm, full stop, together), giving an Arabic gloss for every key word in the book.

### Grammar spine

Present continuous for actions happening now (Unit 1) and for people in pictures throughout; comparatives with -er + than (Unit 1) completed by superlatives with the -est (Unit 5); there is/there are + some (Unit 1) and there was/there were (Unit 6); possessive 's and Whose …? (Units 1 and 3); present simple for routines, timetables and facts, with times of day and telling the time (Unit 2); imperatives and Don't … for rules (Units 1, 5); the past simple carried first by the Lesson 8 stories and emails, then taught explicitly in Unit 6 (went, saw, had, did, played; was/were; did-questions; There was/were); going to for plans (Unit 5); wh- question words systematised (Unit 6); have got with some/any (Unit 7); must/mustn't (Unit 7); plus sequencers (first, then, after that, finally), -ly adverbs, ordinals 1st–4th, and numbers to 1000 (Unit 2) and 3000 (Unit 5).

### Honest negatives

- **No present perfect** as a taught tense: *have got* appears only inside the Unit 7 rhyme and the Unit 8 story (*Has everyone got a bike? — I haven't*), never as a perfect-tense paradigm. No passive, no conditionals, no reported speech, and no *will* future beyond the single dialogue line "Yes, I'll try" (Unit 2) — the future is carried entirely by *going to*.
- **Past-simple negatives and questions are thin**: did-questions occur in the Unit 6 email task and rhyme, but there is no didn't, and no past-tense verb table anywhere in the pupil book; irregular forms are met word by word (went, saw, had, was/were, ran, bought, ate, drank, made, came, got).
- **Phonics is nearly absent**: apart from the Unit 8 pronunciation board game ('ch', 'sh', 'ph', 'gh', 'ee'), the book teaches no letter–sound system and no spelling rules; handwriting is assumed from Grades 1–3.
- **Copy/scan notes:** the file is a clean, complete colour scan; no pages are missing. The dictionary's last spreads are scanned with facing pages reversed (see the offset note), a binding/scan artefact of the Arabic-ordered dictionary section, not lost content. The Iraqi Museum / rivers mini-project on p. 67 is printed with three deliberately wrong words (desert, high, cave) for pupils to find and correct — a teaching device as printed, not a misprint. No ISBN is printed anywhere in this copy.

The remaining leaves (PDF pp. 109–120) are unnumbered cut-out appendices: word cards, picture cards and Game circles 1–4 — the game circles bound in the scrambled order 3, 1, 2, 4.
