## Grade 9 — English for Iraq, 3rd Intermediate Teacher's Book (teacher's book)

### Identity block

- **Identity verdict vs the record title ("English for Iraq — Teacher's Guide"):** MATCH in substance — the genuine Garnet teacher's book for the third intermediate year; the book calls itself "Teacher's Book", not "Teacher's Guide".
- **Title as printed (title page, PDF p. 1):** *English for Iraq — 3rd Intermediate Teacher's Book*, Republic of Iraq, Ministry of Education; imprint Garnet Education. This file has no separate front cover — the title page is the first page.
- **Authors as printed:** Terry O'Neill and Peter Snow (the Student's Book of the set adds Sally Etherton). The imprint page repeats the two author biographies (O'Neill: Dublin, Dar-es-Salaam and Bahrain, Essex MA 1972, BBC *English by Radio and Television*; Snow: London University modern languages, Germany and the Netherlands, then the BBC). The Ministry acknowledgements thank Hanaa Adil Ali, Tawadud Mohammed Ridha, Hind Farooq Ali, Omar Osamah Nashaat, Nada Kadhim Hussein and Khulud Aswad Shmes for their work in support of the Editorial and Adaptation Committee.
- **Publisher:** Garnet Publishing Ltd., 8 Southern Court, South Street, Reading RG1 4QS, UK.
- **Edition/year as printed (imprint, PDF p. 2):** "First edition, copyright © 2016 Garnet Publishing Ltd." Production slug as printed: "IRQ15 TB 3rdI". **This file is the first edition (2016), not the 2023 revised edition** of the Student's Book in this set — see the honest negatives for the consequences (page references and some contents follow first-edition pagination).
- **ISBN as printed:** no ISBN printed — none on the title page or imprint page, and this file contains no back cover.
- **PDF pages:** 161, all read.
- **Printed vs PDF offset:** the book's folios run 1–160 in the footer ("English for Iraq Teacher's Book 3rd Intermediate: <section>"), counting the title page as p. 1 and the imprint as p. 2, with text from printed p. 3. PDF page = printed page as far as printed p. 155; **printed p. 155 is scanned twice** (PDF pp. 155 and 156), so from printed p. 156 onward PDF page = printed + 1, ending at printed p. 160 (a blank page) = PDF p. 161.
- **Text-layer character:** none — page images only; read whole from 4-up contact sheets. No third-party wrapper or watermark stamps in this copy (unlike the Grade 7 teacher's book in this set).

### Structure and unit-by-unit summary

**Introduction (printed pp. 3–31).** Section A, "About English for Iraq, Intermediate stage": components and exercise types; the year's organisation — **68 lessons**: core units (1–3, 5–7) have ten lessons each, revision Units 4 and 8 have four lessons each plus tests; Lesson 10 is extensive reading with Story time; the AB Extra activities can be set as test material; the abbreviations key (SB5, AB7, TB12, TP L1/S1/V1/R1 …). A distinctive feature of this year is set out here: a single **Storytelling project** runs through the whole course — the project pages at SB 82–87 are worked in 16 stages distributed across the units (a table maps Activities 1–16 to Units 1, 2, 3, 5, 6 and 7 and to their writing tasks, from "Categorize the elements of a good story" in Unit 1 to drafting and presenting group stories), and each unit's summary below notes its project stages. Section A also gives a unit-by-unit content overview with each unit's grammatical areas, and a full account of the Storytelling project (genres: an imaginary tale of two brothers and magic, mystery, science fiction, adventure and human endeavour/sport; groups produce two complete stories). Section B, "Overview of main grammatical areas covered in English for Iraq" (pp. 9–14): the cumulative grammar inventory of the whole series to date — adjectives and comparison, defining adjective clauses, adverbs, adverbial clauses of reason and time (*when* + past + past; *when* + past continuous + past), adverbial phrases, conjunctions and link words, determiners and articles, *some/any/a few/a little/a lot of/too many/too much*, gerunds, imperatives, infinitives, modals (*have/have got, have to, can, should, must, may/might*), nouns, possessives (including *s* apostrophe — "men's/women's/children's bags"), prepositions, pronouns (including reflexives), the full punctuation system, question forms, simple/compound/complex sentences, the verb pattern *want + noun/pronoun + to + infinitive*, and the tense system: present simple, present continuous (actions and future arrangements), present perfect (recent actions, experience, duration with *for/since*), past simple, past continuous, past habitual (*used to*), future (*will*, present continuous, *going to*), future continuous, Type 1 conditionals, and the passive (present simple, past simple, with modal *can*). Section C, "Advice to teachers" (pp. 15–18): the classroom, pair and group work (with a fuller discussion of organising group work for the project), mixed abilities, lesson planning, classroom language, using Arabic, and assessment — which names the AB 'Check your progress' sections in Units 4 and 8 and ongoing assessment. Section D, "Teaching techniques and procedures" (pp. 19–30): the integrated-skills approach; TP L1 task-listening; speaking objectives (with more less-guided speaking than earlier books) and TP S1; vocabulary objectives and the key-words system, TP V1 and TP V2 (working out meaning from context, including visual clues); text types; reading techniques (predicting, skimming, search reading, scanning) with TP R1–R4; writing objectives and procedures TP W1/W2 (continuous writing built on topic sentences and paragraph organisation; expanding notes; correcting teacher-marked paragraphs); dictation TP D1 (no dictation exercises in the book); spelling, punctuation, language work and correction policy. Section E, "Project work" (p. 31): the Storytelling project's aims, benefits and five stages. A two-page **Book map** (pp. 32–33) reproduces the Student's Book map with the Storytelling strand shown under each unit.

As in the other teacher's books, each unit's notes follow the pattern: **Unit Summary** table, **Unit objectives**, step-by-step **lesson notes** with Objectives/Language/Vocabulary boxes, full **transcripts**, **answers**, and Extra activities answers. Unlike the Grade 7 book there are no per-unit word lists in the notes; the key words are collected in one end-of-book list.

**Unit 1 — Describing things (notes pp. 34–52).** The lessons revise and build description language: the mall (*There is/are …, It has …*); telling the time in three ways with present/past continuous in the phone conversations, plus close work on spoken language (*just, then, right, really*, "See you in a minute"); the Panther 3.0D article (guessing words in context; adjectives and the nouns they describe; modifiers and intensifiers *quite, very, really*); buying and selling (*so you can/can't …, to help you to …*; compound nouns); the language of adverts and writing an email about a new item; describing people (appearance) and, in Lesson 7, character — with polite negative descriptions; Lesson 8 is a project lesson in the first-edition layout (designing an advert from the toothbrush information on SB12, using comparatives); Round up; Story time "Lucky customer 50"; Extra activities answers (AB18–19 in first-edition pagination). Project activity 1 (SB82): the elements of a good story — plot devices (conflict and resolution, betrayal, mistaken identity, overheard conversation, rise and fall), structural devices (beginning, middle and end) and language features (adjectives, adverbs, exclamative sentences).

**Unit 2 — Spare time activities (pp. 54–70).** Likes and dislikes with gerunds and the like-scale; suggestions and preferences (*to go/go/going*); book genres and completing a book review (past simple for narrative, including the passive); the Lucy Fisher interview (present simple vs past simple questions; correcting nine information mistakes and eight spelling mistakes); the two film conversations (ellipsis in informal speech); the STAR! article (compound and complex sentences; telling a story in the present, then retelling in the past); invitations (transferring information to a table); a fact file lesson — in the first edition the model fact file is *Titanic*, and students write and quiz on their own (the 2023 Student's Book uses *The Greatest Showman*); Round up; Lesson 10 "What is your hobby?" (reading for gist and detail; guessing words from context). Project activities 2–4 (SB82–83): reading *The Stork-Ruler* and identifying the story elements in it — Nurredin, the ruler turned into a stork, betrayal by his brother.

**Unit 3 — Our world (pp. 72–87).** Comparisons in full ((not) as … as; comparative spelling rules) and a guessing game; headlines and the first conditional (numbers, rates of change); feelings and agreement (So/Neither) with the second conditional introduced inside the Mithaq and Mushtaq spider conversation (the notes' names — see negatives); the second conditional taught formally (Lesson 4), with Arabic expressly permitted to explain the tense contrast; the falconry text (inference; pronouns and possessive adjectives, including *its* vs *it's*); the wildlife questionnaire (its structure analysed; punctuation of conditional questions); Iraq's animals listening and the present passive (including past passive forms in the AB); the Marsh Arabs fact file and writing a parallel fact file; Round up; Lesson 10 (SB37) — a nature reading, "Iraq's first national park / Things happen in nature for a reason": two linked stories (a hunter blinded by lightning who shoots at a lion; farmers, monkeys and leopards) answered through the AB summary and vocabulary work. Project activities 5–6 (SB84–85): story openers in two genres (horror/scary and science fiction) and planning the group stories.

**Unit 4 — Review (pp. 88–97).** Four skills lessons following the Student's Book exactly: Listening (tips 1–3 with the full announcement transcripts), Reading (tips 1–4), Speaking (tips 1–4), Writing (tips 1–4, ending in a complete worked reconstruction of a *Treasure Island* summary — plan, topic sentences and model text are all printed as the answer key). Then **Tests A–C (AB51–58 in first-edition pagination)**: Test A, listening and vocabulary (the missing-girl announcement, the description ticking exercise, and the "Best Cars" Panther 4.5 advertisement, with transcripts and full answer keys for Questions A–G); Test B, a reading test on the "Ibrahim's life story" text (paragraph headings, true/false, and *Because* answers); Test C, a writing test with a complete marking scheme (Plan 20, Topic sentences 20, Information 20, Sentences 20, First draft 5, Editing 10, Final text 5); and a "Return test" lesson on learning from marked tests.

**Unit 5 — The media (pp. 98–115).** Watching the match (past simple question forms; the three match conversations with scores: Brazil 9 – India 0, Juventus 2 – AC Milan 2, Manchester United 3 – Arsenal 1, as keyed); the Nawal Ramzi interview (background events vs storyline — past continuous against past simple); the newspaper lessons (scanning for numerals; sections and contents; the Salma/John story) with subject and object pronouns; the graduation party reviews (forming negatives); "Would you take less?" (idioms and question forms of buying and selling; both Khaled's-father calls in full); the activity-week timetable (future simple *will*); the survey lesson (*most, some, a few*; tally and bar charts); Round up; Story time "A powerful lesson for everyone" with acting the story in groups. Project activities 7–10 (SB85): making the language of the group stories more exciting (adjectives, adverbs, similes — spider diagrams of descriptive vocabulary), sharing paragraphs and structural devices, and presenting stories to the class.

**Unit 6 — Jobs and careers (pp. 116–129).** Jobs and workplaces named and described with defining relative clauses (*someone who …*); the language game and writing about wanted and unwanted jobs; careers advice — the Layla and Dania headteacher interviews in full (*going to* for plans vs *will*), pronunciation practice and role play; "The school of the future" (defining relative clauses with *who, which, that*; combining clauses); articles *a, an, the* and zero article studied from the two conversations and applied in two paragraphs (Tikrit/Cambridge; Erbil/Mosul); directions (the Mr Saad bookshop conversation in full; role-playing with home-drawn maps); "A famous career" — Ibn al-Haitham (predicting paragraph topics; search-reading; link phrases also/and/but/which/who); the school project lesson (reconstructing Faisal's questions from Bob Pearson's answers; polite question forms; planning an own career interview); Round up; Lesson 10 (SB65) — "What should I do?": reading Mona's blog and writing a reply, with the AB's "Ali's career" text answered in the Extra activities key. Project activities 11–12 (SB86–87): adventure and sports story openers, and final planning of the second group stories.

**Unit 7 — Other countries (pp. 130–143).** Souvenirs and the present perfect; nationality adjectives and writing about oneself; customs texts scanned (time adverbials — definite and indefinite); travel adventures (definite and zero article with geographical features — the *the* table for continents, countries, waterways, lakes, deserts, mountains, groups and cities); adverbs and adverbials with the word-order language game; apologising (past simple vs present perfect reviewed in the language box); **in the first edition's order**, Lesson 6 is "Two African countries" (Libya and Sudan — with the notes giving Libya's area as 1,759,541 km² against Sudan's 612,184 km² and populations of 6,244,174 and 12,681,647 as printed there), Lesson 7 is the Asian Games (read for interest, with the medal table used for scanning practice), and Lesson 8 is "How many mobile phones are produced every day?" (present and past passive, including a note on the present continuous passive in "new technology is being developed"); Round up; Lesson 10 (SB77) — "Life is like a cup of coffee", a moral story about university alumni, read for pleasure with a storyline summary in the AB. Project activities 13–16 (SB87): final language work on the stories and presenting them. Extra activities answers (AB98–101) include a Thor Heyerdahl text (his beliefs, building the boat *Tigris*, the journey, his letter) that does not appear in the 2023 Activity Book in this set.

**Unit 8 — Review (pp. 144–151).** Four lessons: Listening for language and feelings (Listening tip 4; the three track-33 conversations in full — Hamid and Jassim, John Green and Mr Pearson, Fadia/Noura/Haya and the class magazine — with the tense-meaning analysis: *I would help you* vs *I will help you*, present continuous with present and future meanings); Speaking (the question-bank game over the whole book, plus the jobs language game from SB57); Reading (strategies applied to a story about a girl called Rafal and a cat on SB80 — a text that is not in the 2023 Student's Book, where the lesson works on the Daily Arab News page); Writing (correcting the 17 marked errors in the falconry text and ordering its paragraphs — the full correction key is printed). Then **Tests A–C (AB102–111)**: Test A, listening and grammar (Tariq Rahdi's wildlife-photographer talk in full, note headings, article and question-formation keys); Test B, a reading test on the Daily Arab News articles (preparation, detail and direct/less-direct/wh- question keys); Test C, a writing test with its marking guide (an impression mark plus specific marks for accuracy, variety of structure, variety of vocabulary and handwriting).

**Key words (pp. 152–157).** The end-of-book word list, grouped by unit and alphabetical within the unit, with the notation (n) noun, (v) verb, (adj) adjective, (pt) past tense, (pl) plural, and comparative/superlative forms shown for Unit 1 adjectives (big – bigger/biggest; expensive – less expensive/least expensive/more expensive/most expensive). It runs from Unit 1 "artistic" to Unit 8 "nevertheless". Ruled/blank Notes pages (pp. 158–160) close the book; the final printed page (160) is blank.

### Grammar spine (the year's new language, per the unit notes)

*There is/are* and *It has …* for places, with adjective order and the modifiers *very/quite/really* (Unit 1) → present/past continuous contrast for narrative (Unit 1) → purpose clauses with *so you can …* and *to help you to …* (Unit 1) → compound words (Unit 1) → verbs + gerund/infinitive and compound and complex sentences (Unit 2) → comparatives, superlatives and *(not) as … as* (Unit 3) → the first conditional (Unit 3) → **the second conditional** (Unit 3) → past habitual *used to* (Unit 3) → present simple passive with *can* (Unit 3) → past simple vs past continuous as background vs storyline, subject/object pronouns, future *will* (Unit 5) → *going to* vs *will*, defining relative clauses with *who/which/that*, the article system (Unit 6) → adverbials and their word order, present perfect vs past simple, present and past passive (Unit 7).

### Honest negatives

- **Edition skew against the other Grade 9 books in this set (the material caveat for this filing):** this Teacher's Book is the 2016 first edition written for the first-edition Student's/Activity Books, while the set's Student's Book is the 2023 revised edition and its Activity Book is the 2022/2023 production. Its SB/AB page references therefore follow first-edition pagination and drift from the revised books (e.g. its Unit 3 lessons sit at SB26–37 against SB30–43 in the revised book), its end-of-semester tests are keyed to Activity Book pages (51–58 and 102–111) that do not exist in the revised Activity Book in this set, and several contents differ: the Unit 7 lesson order (Two African countries and the Asian Games are Lessons 6 and 7 here), a *Titanic* fact file where the revised book has *The Greatest Showman*, a Unit 3 Lesson 10 national-park reading and a Unit 7 "Life is like a cup of coffee" story that the revised Student's Book does not contain (its Unit 3 story is "How to fill a space" and its Unit 7 story "Together for a better school"), a Rafal-and-the-cat story in Unit 8, a Heyerdahl extra-activities text, 'Check your progress' AB sections named in the assessment advice, and spider-boy names Mithaq/Mushtaq here against Kareem/Rami in the revised Activity Book. The language syllabus, unit themes and nearly all texts are otherwise the same course.
- Scan defects in this copy: **printed p. 53** (the Unit 2 Summary's opening page) is rotated and cropped at the corner, partly obscuring the table's first rows (the missing rows are recoverable from p. 54); **printed p. 155 is scanned twice** (PDF pp. 155–156), so the file has one duplicate page and no folio is missing. The file contains no front or back cover.
- The Introduction's own overview says Unit 5's Lesson 2 interview illustrates the past-tense contrast through "a life-changing event" and calls the Unit 6 reading text a career history of "the scientist Ibn al-Haitham" — both consistent with the notes; no internal contradictions beyond the edition differences above were found.
- The book assumes the course audio (34 tracks) throughout; the audio itself is not part of this file. There is no consolidated transcript section (transcripts sit inside the lesson notes, as in the Grade 8 book) and no per-unit word lists in the notes (one end-of-book list instead).

## Grade 10 — English for Iraq, 4th Preparatory Student's Book (Student's Book)

Record title it was filed under: "English for Iraq — Student's Book" (Grade 10). **Verdict: the file is that book.** The cover title of the whole series is *English for Iraq*; the stage name as printed is "4th Preparatory" (= Grade 10, the first year of the Iraqi preparatory stage).

### Identity block

- **Identity verdict vs the record:** Match — Grade 10 Student's Book of *English for Iraq*; the record's generic title omits the printed stage name "4th Preparatory", which the book itself carries on the title page.
- **Title as printed (title page, PDF p. 1):** *English for Iraq — 4th Preparatory Student's Book*, under the masthead of the Republic of Iraq, Ministry of Education, General Directorate of Curricula.
- **Authors as printed (title page):** Caroline de Messieres and Edward Alden. The title page also names an Editorial and Adaptation Committee (Malek Mohamed Juda, Head of the English Curriculum; Adil Ali Moussa; Muayyad Naji Ahmed; Ramzi Nouri Abdullah) and a Revision Committee (Hind Farooq Ali and others).
- **Publisher:** Garnet Education / Garnet Publishing Ltd, 8 Southern Court, South Street, Reading RG1 4QS, UK (imprint, PDF p. 138).
- **Edition / year as printed (imprint, PDF p. 138):** First edition copyright © 2014 Garnet Publishing Ltd; Second edition © 2019; **Revised edition © 2023** — the held copy is the 2023 revised edition (production code printed on the credits page: "IRQ23 SB 4thP").
- **ISBN:** No ISBN printed anywhere in this file (title page and imprint/credits page carry none).
- **PDF pages:** 138, all read.
- **Printed vs PDF offset:** **Printed page = PDF page (offset 0)** wherever a folio is printed. PDF p. 1 is the unnumbered title page; the Book Map is printed pp. 2–3; unit divider pages (PDF pp. 4–5, 40, 46–47, 59, 71, 82–83, 99) carry no folio; the last printed folio is p. 137 (Transcripts), and PDF p. 138 is the unnumbered imprint/credits page. All page references below are to the printed pages.
- **Text-layer character:** No text layer at all — the PDF is page images only (0 extractable characters); read whole from 4-up contact sheets of page renders. The copy is a scan of a used printed book: a previous owner's handwritten name ("Abbas3rd") appears on the title page, on p. 95 and on the credits page, and one word is crossed out in pen on p. 86.
- **Cast/design:** Full-colour coursebook; eight units colour-coded, each Student's Book lesson cross-referenced to Activity Book pages ("AB") printed in the lesson tab. No front or back cover is included in the file — it opens at the title page and ends at the credits page.

### Unit-by-unit summary

**Book Map (pp. 2–3).** A five-column syllabus grid for all eight units: lexical areas, communicative activities, language-learning activities, grammatical areas and pronunciation/phonology — the book's own complete map of what follows.

**Unit 1 — Language learning (pp. 6–16).** Lesson 1 "How do you learn?" presents language-learning methods and equipment (language school, self-access centre, bilingual dictionary, exchange programme, index cards, learning diary) with the present simple for habits and facts. Lesson 2 "Changes" teaches topic sentences and matching paragraphs to graphs, with the present continuous for trends (numbers of language learners rising/falling). Lesson 3 "Choosing a summer programme" is a leaflet-reading lesson with adjectives ending in *-ed* and *-ing*. Lesson 4 "So do I!" matches teenagers to UK summer schools and practises agreeing/disagreeing. Lesson 5 "Asking for information" teaches indirect questions (*Could you tell me …?*) and filling in an application form. Lesson 6 "A tour of the self-access centre" practises imperatives and directions. Lesson 7 "Meeting and greeting" works on greetings and introductions at a book fair. Lesson 8 "Arabic and English" combines a student's talk notes on Arabic (speakers, dialects, history, the Holy Qur'an) with an article on why English has so many synonyms. Lesson 9 "Official languages" matches countries to official languages and reads about bilingual Canada. A Round up revision page (p. 16) closes the unit.

**Unit 2 — Education (pp. 18–28).** Lesson 1 "What do you do at school?" covers school activities and subjects, a school timetable, and *lend* vs *borrow*. Lesson 2 "Two students" compares two boys' schools in Oman and Canada, teaching ways of saying things are the same or different (*both … and, neither … nor, as … as, like/unlike, not as … as*). Lesson 3 "Helping people learn" (pp. 20–21) is a long UNICEF text on why millions of children do not go to school (poverty, child labour, destroyed schools) plus a charity appeal text. Lesson 4 "Denise's story" is a picture story about a girl who had to leave school to pick coffee and was helped back by an organisation (obligation: *have to / don't have to*). Lesson 5 "School memories" listens to older men describing school sixty years ago and teaches adjective + infinitive (*fun to ride, easy to do*). Lesson 6 "A special school" reads the website of a school for child actors, musicians and athletes, with past simple and past continuous. Lesson 7 "An interesting job" follows a tutor to child film actors and teaches past perfect simple and continuous. Lesson 8 "Sami writes an essay" models a school essay (a pet monkey escapes in school) and its sequencing words. Lesson 9 "An interview" has a student write the questions for an interview with her teacher. Round up p. 28.

**Unit 3 — Travel (pp. 30–39).** Lesson 1 "Places to go" matches famous sites (the Great Pyramids of Giza, the Hanging Gardens of Babylon, Stonehenge, the Parthenon) to visitor accounts, with adjectives to describe places. Lesson 2 "Choosing a holiday" teaches the present perfect with *ever, never, yet* and *already*. Lesson 3 "Holiday fun" reads about snowboarding, whitewater rafting and quad biking, and teaches *the* with place names (the Alps, the Colorado River, but Chamonix, Saudi Arabia). Lesson 4 "Getting information" is telephone listening (booking flights and a hotel) with checking/confirming phrases. Lesson 5 "What do you need?" works with travel documents (guidebook, leaflet, plane ticket, receipt, timetable) and the present simple for timetables. Lesson 6 "At the hotel" teaches hotel vocabulary (photos of Baghdad hotels) and making requests (*Can I have …? Could I have …? Would you send …?*). Lesson 7 "Where is it?" is directions and proximity on a town map (*around the corner, near, opposite*). Lesson 8 "A guidebook" reads a Rome guidebook (Piazza Navona, the Spanish Steps, the Pantheon, the Roman Forum). Lesson 9 "Writing home" contrasts a postcard from Saudi Arabia with an email from England and teaches the present perfect continuous. Round up p. 39.

**Unit 4 — Review of Units 1–3 (pp. 42–45).** A short four-lesson review unit. Lesson 1 "The gap year" reads a magazine article on gap years (charity work, travel, planning). Lesson 2 "A pilot's job" is a listening interview plus an Iraqi Airways timetable. Lesson 3 "On the phone" compares communication methods and debates the advantages and disadvantages of mobile phones. Lesson 4 "Eid al-Adha" teaches essay planning: a student's notes for an Eid visit essay are turned into ordered paragraphs.

**Unit 5 — Family and friends (pp. 48–58).** Lesson 1 "Describing people" teaches adjectives for appearance and character (bald, dark-complexioned, wavy, tall and slim, bright, sensible, reliable, forgetful). Lesson 2 "Would you like to go to the cinema?" reads three short film reviews, teaches extreme adjectives with intensifying adverbs (absolutely fascinating, extremely rare, incredibly funny) and invitations, offers and promises (*Would you like …? I'll … Shall I …?*). Lesson 3 "A birthday present" is a family dialogue teaching the future simple for decisions, promises and predictions. Lesson 4 "Planning a party" teaches the causative *get/have + object + past participle* (have a cake made, get the invitations printed). Lesson 5 "Wedding customs" reads an article on wedding customs around the world (including henna customs, Bedouin weddings, and the Iraqi bride wearing white) with determiners *few, some, both, all*. Lesson 6 "Welcome Home Week" is a dialogue about Irish emigrants returning home, teaching the future continuous. Lessons 7–8 (pp. 54–56) pair a Lebanon guidebook extract on crafts with an interview with a glass-blower whose family business passes the skill from father to son, and a "Special events" lesson on cards and invitations with verb + *to* + infinitive. Lesson 9 "A school trip" is a first-person account of a school visit to the National Museum of Iraq in Baghdad (Assyrian and Babylonian galleries, cuneiform, the Lamassu). Round up p. 58.

**Unit 6 — Health and fitness (pp. 60–70).** Lesson 1 "A healthy lunch" is a nutritionist's talk on food groups, vitamins and calories. Lesson 2 "Have a good night!" is a magazine article on sleep and teenagers, used to teach reported speech (*ask, tell, say*). Lesson 3 "How do you feel?" (pp. 62–63) is illness dialogues (sore throat, headache, fever, flu, sprained ankle, sunburn) with advice modals *should/shouldn't, ought to, could*. Lesson 4 "A check-up" follows a tired schoolboy at the doctor. Lesson 5 "At the chemist's" teaches ailment vocabulary (a sunburn, a cough, a sore throat, a headache, a cut, a sprain, a fever) through listening. Lesson 6 "Take my advice!" is a fitness trainer's talk on starting exercise safely, with prefixes and suffixes. Lesson 7 "What do you think will happen?" uses future newspaper headlines to teach *may/might* for possible events. Lesson 8 "The FIFA World Cup" is an article on the tournament's history and format, including Qatar 2022 and the 2026 expansion to 48 teams. Lesson 9 "Healthy body, healthy mind!" reads a National Fitness Day webpage. Round up p. 70.

**Unit 7 — Science and technology (pp. 72–81).** Lesson 1 "Meet the scientists" matches job descriptions to science jobs (meteorologist, geologist, aerospace engineer, marine biologist, forester). Lesson 2 "Winds" is a science-magazine article (pressure, trade winds, the Shamal, hurricanes) teaching the zero conditional. Lesson 3 "A weather forecast" works with an international forecast text and the first conditional. Lesson 4 "Restoring the Garden of Eden" reads about the Al-Hammaar Marshes of southern Iraq, their draining in the 1990s and their restoration, with adverbs of certainty (definitely, probably, perhaps). Lesson 5 "From the Earth to the refinery" explains how petroleum forms and is refined, teaching the passive. Lesson 6 "Online shopping" is a for-and-against text on e-commerce. Lesson 7 "A presentation" orders a student's presentation about satellites and teaches signposting language. Lesson 8 "The International Space Station" practises presentation phrases on an ISS text. Lesson 9 "A science report" reads a report on the endangered hawksbill sea turtle and teaches contrasting linkers (*however, although, but*). Round up p. 81.

**Unit 8 — Review of Units 5–7 (pp. 84–87).** Four lessons: "GPS receivers" (a text explaining how GPS works), "Keeping fit in space" (an interview with an astronaut about exercise, bones and sleep in zero gravity), "What's the job for you?" (a magazine quiz matching interests to jobs) and "Would you like to go out?" (two invitation dialogues practising the whole course's functions).

**Grammar and Functions Reference (pp. 88–98).** Unit-by-unit form-and-use tables for every grammar point in the book, ending (p. 98) with the phonemic alphabet chart and a list of irregular verbs.

**Literature Focus (pp. 100–115).** Two graded readers, each introduced by "About the author / Importance of the book / The story" pages and then read in three sections, each section pairing a "The story so far …" summary box with an extract and a glossary. *Treasure Island* (pp. 100–107): Stevenson's novel in summary and extract — Billy Bones' map, the voyage of the Hispaniola, Ben Gunn, and the empty treasure hole. *Around the World in 80 Days* (pp. 108–115): Verne's novel — Phileas Fogg's wager at the Reform Club, Detective Fix's pursuit, and the day gained by travelling east that wins the bet.

**Transcripts (pp. 116–137).** Full transcripts of all 79 audio tracks, Units 1–8 plus a Unit 3 Revision set. The book ends with an unnumbered imprint/acknowledgements/photography-credits page (PDF p. 138).

### Grammar spine

Present simple (habits/facts; also for timetables) and present continuous (actions now, changing trends, planned futures) in Unit 1, with indirect questions and imperatives; comparison structures (both/neither, as … as, like/unlike), adjective + infinitive, past simple and past continuous, and past perfect simple and continuous in Unit 2; present perfect (ever/never/yet/already), articles with place names, polite requests and the present perfect continuous in Unit 3; Unit 4 reviews Units 1–3. Unit 5 adds future simple, causative get/have something done, determiners (few/some/both/all), future continuous and verb + to + infinitive; Unit 6 adds reported speech, advice modals (should, ought to, could), may/might for possibility and affixes; Unit 7 adds zero and first conditionals, adverbs of certainty, the passive in several tenses, and contrast linkers (however/although/but); Unit 8 reviews Units 5–7. A back-of-book Grammar and Functions Reference (pp. 88–98) tabulates every point.

## Grade 10 — English for Iraq, 4th Preparatory Activity Book (Activity Book)

Record title it was filed under: "English for Iraq — Activity Book" (Grade 10). **Verdict: the file is that book** — the workbook companion to the Grade 10 (4th Preparatory) Student's Book.

### Identity block

- **Identity verdict vs the record:** Match — the Grade 10 Activity Book of *English for Iraq*, stage name as printed "4th Preparatory".
- **Title as printed (title page, PDF p. 1):** *English for Iraq — 4th Preparatory Activity Book*, under the masthead of the Republic of Iraq, Ministry of Education, General Directorate of Curricula. The title page also carries the ministry's Arabic notice that the book is distributed free by law and must not be sold.
- **Authors as printed (title page):** Caroline de Messieres and Edward Alden; Editorial and Adaptation Committee and Revision Committee as in the Student's Book.
- **Publisher:** Garnet Education / Garnet Publishing Ltd, 8 Southern Court, South Street, Reading RG1 4QS, UK (imprint page, PDF p. 2).
- **Edition / year as printed (imprint, PDF p. 2):** First edition copyright © 2015 Garnet Publishing Ltd; **Revised edition © 2023** — the held copy is the 2023 revised edition (production code "IRQ23 AB 4thP").
- **ISBN:** No ISBN printed anywhere in this file.
- **PDF pages:** 154, all read.
- **Printed vs PDF offset:** **Printed page = PDF page (offset 0).** PDF p. 1 is the unnumbered title page, PDF p. 2 the unnumbered imprint page; the first folio is p. 3 ("This book belongs to …") and folios then run unbroken to p. 154 (a ruled Notes page). All page references below are to the printed pages.
- **Text-layer character:** No text layer — page images only; read whole from 4-up contact sheets. Unlike the colour Student's Book, the Activity Book is printed in **black and white / greyscale** throughout.
- **Cast/design:** A write-in workbook: every Student's Book lesson has a parallel Activity Book lesson headed "Unit N / Lesson M: <same title>", and most full units close with a two-page **Revision** section and a two-page **Test**. Exercises are keyed to the shared course audio by the same track numbers as the Student's Book transcripts. The scan carries a handwritten source mark — "telegram: abbas3rd" — at the top of p. 4, at the foot of p. 99, and on the final Notes page (p. 154), showing the file circulated via that Telegram account; the exercises themselves are unfilled in this copy.

### Unit-by-unit summary

**Unit 1 — Language learning (pp. 4–25).** Lesson-by-lesson written practice for the Student's Book unit: listening true/false and present-simple verb forms (L1, pp. 4–5, with a dictionary-entry anatomy exercise and an alphabetical-order exercise); topic sentences and present-continuous forms and uses (L2, pp. 6–7); *-ed/-ing* adjectives (L3, pp. 8–9); agreeing and disagreeing responses and /iː/ vs /e/ sounds (L4, pp. 10–11); form-filling language — surname, DOB, date formats, Iraq's country code 964 — plus completing a real application form and word-order practice for indirect questions (L5, pp. 12–13); directions phrases and a guided description of the self-access centre (L6, pp. 14–15); greeting/introduction conversation completion (L7, pp. 16–17); note-taking abbreviations (e.g., esp., langs, yrs), synonyms and antonyms, and a research-and-write task about Arabic (L8, pp. 18–19); and locating answers in the Canada assignment text, definitions, and guided notes for a paragraph about Iraq (L9, pp. 20–21). The unit closes with **Revision** (pp. 22–23) and a **Test** (pp. 24–25) built on a four-paragraph text about Pakistan.

**Unit 2 — Education (pp. 26–47).** School-activity vocabulary and *borrow/lend* with a timetable-completion listening (L1, pp. 26–27); plural *-s* pronunciation (/z/ vs /ɪz/) and a comparison reading about two new boys, Abdulla and Liam, practising *both … and, neither … nor, as … as, unlike* (L2, pp. 28–29); UNICEF text questions and *have to / don't have to* (L3, pp. 30–31); conjunctions *and, or, because, so* (L4, p. 33); *could/couldn't* from the school-memories listening, adjective + infinitive, and an "interview a relative" writing frame (L5, pp. 34–35); the Meade School text completion and past simple vs past continuous, including a gapped story and question word-order (L6, pp. 36–37); past perfect simple and continuous in two gapped day-in-the-life texts (L7, pp. 38–39); ordering the events of Sami's monkey essay and writing a sequenced story of one's own (L8, pp. 40–41); and writing interview questions from given answers, ending in a mock job interview and a decision paragraph (L9, pp. 42–43). **Revision** pp. 44–45; **Test** pp. 46–47 (a surprise-birthday-party story, past-tense cloze, and a picture-prompted story).

**Unit 3 — Travel (pp. 48–69).** Place-name and site vocabulary from Sandra's travel notes (L1, pp. 48–49); *Have you ever …?* questions and *never / already / yet* (L2, pp. 50–51); *the* with geographical names, including an error-deletion paragraph about Australia and a caving text mentioning Einchki Cave in Duhok (L3, pp. 52–53); checking-information language in the airline and hotel phone calls (L4, pp. 54–55); completing an Egypt tour itinerary from travel documents, /eɪ/ vs /æ/ sounds, and the present simple for timetables (L5, pp. 56–57); hotel and restaurant request dialogues (L6, pp. 58–59); directions and proximity phrases on the town map (L7, pp. 60–61); describing-place adjectives (crowded, wide, triangular, ancient) and writing about traditional places in Iraq (L8, pp. 62–63); and *already/yet* with the present perfect continuous from pictures, finishing with planning and writing a postcard or email (L9, pp. 64–65). **Revision** pp. 66–67; **Test** pp. 68–69 (directions on a Baghdad street map — Al Risafi Street / Al Rasheed Street — and a verb-form letter).

**Unit 4 — Review (pp. 70–75).** The workbook mirrors the Student's Book's short review unit and has **no Revision section**: gap-year article questions and the present continuous for change (L1, p. 70); the pilot interview and *have to* sentences, plus researching a job (L2, p. 71); advantages/disadvantages tables for mobile phones, postcards, letters and email (L3, p. 72); correcting five planted mistakes in the Eid al-Adha essay paragraph and writing two paragraphs about an event (L4, p. 73); then a two-page **Test** (pp. 74–75) on present perfect simple vs continuous, sentence correction, travel vocabulary and past simple vs continuous.

**Unit 5 — Family and friends (pp. 76–97).** Sorting appearance vs character adjectives and present perfect simple/continuous forms from the Lina dialogue (L1, pp. 76–77); invitations, offers and promises — matching, unscrambling and reply-matching (L2, pp. 78–79); future simple with *will/won't* (L3, pp. 80–81); the causative *get/have something done* in party-planning dialogues and a partner "to do" list task (L4, pp. 82–83); wedding vocabulary (bride, groom, ceremony, henna, mother-in-law), determiners *all, both, either, few, some*, and a 75–100-word paragraph on wedding customs in Iraq (L5, pp. 84–85); the future continuous, including a Scottish homecoming paragraph (kilts, haggis, Burns Night) and writing about one's own homecoming (L6, pp. 86–87); glass-blowing comprehension and strong adjectives (very good → excellent, etc.) (L7, pp. 88–89); verb + *to* + infinitive sentence endings (L8, pp. 90–91); and National Museum of Iraq comprehension with a gapped summary of the school-trip text (L9, pp. 92–93). **Revision** pp. 94–95 includes key-word sentence transformations (SHALL, GETTING, ONLY, WORKING, REFUSED, LIKE); **Test** pp. 96–97 is multiple choice plus causative and determiner completion.

**Unit 6 — Health and fitness (pp. 98–119).** Nutrition vocabulary (calcium, calories, dairy products, protein, vitamins) and quantifiers (*too much, too many, enough, fewer*), ending in a 90–100-word paragraph on eating habits (L1, pp. 98–99); reported statements **and reported questions** built from the sleep article, then converting reported speech back into direct speech (L2, pp. 100–101); an ailment collocation table (have a sore throat/fever/flu; sprain/break your ankle) and writing advice with *should* (L3, pp. 102–103); the doctor–patient dialogue completed and role-played, and a 50–75-word paragraph about one's lifestyle (L4, pp. 104–105); chemist's-shop listening questions and rewriting customers' words in reported speech (L5, pp. 106–107); the fitness talk's paragraph topics and prefix/suffix word-building over a short text about bullet journals (L6, pp. 108–109); *might/might not* predictions with reasons (L7, pp. 110–111); World Cup true/false and vocabulary from the Student's Book article, plus researching and writing a paragraph about a sporting event (L8, pp. 112–113); and an Activity-Book-only article, "5 benefits of exercise for the mind", with gist, detail and vocabulary work (L9, pp. 114–115). **Revision** pp. 116–117 (a vocabulary-clue quiz game); **Test** pp. 118–119 (reported-speech completion, *might* sentences, and a "Why exercise?" reading).

**Unit 7 — Science and technology (pp. 120–141).** Science-job definitions and general science vocabulary (atmosphere, laboratory, oxygen, pollute, radar map, satellite, test tube) (L1, pp. 120–121); zero-conditional correction, completion and free writing ("If the Shamal blows, …") (L2, pp. 122–123); first-conditional choice and completion, and writing an email about tomorrow's possible plans (L3, pp. 124–125); Al-Hammaar Marshes questions and the sentence positions of *probably, perhaps, definitely* (L4, pp. 126–127); ordering the petroleum process and hunting four passive forms in the refinery text, then passive formation across five tenses (L5, pp. 128–129); online-shopping vocabulary and writing a summary of the Student's Book text (L6, pp. 130–131); presentation signposting phrases and sentence-stress marking (L7, pp. 132–133); pausing in delivery, then researching, preparing and peer-assessing a full presentation about a planet (L8, pp. 134–135); finding and fixing five planted mistakes in a summary of the hawksbill report, *however/although/but* matching, and writing a report about a marine animal (L9, pp. 136–137). **Revision** pp. 138–139; **Test** pp. 140–141 (five passive forms, unit vocabulary, collocations, and an Activity-Book-only reading text about Saturn and the Cassini mission).

**Unit 8 — Review (pp. 142–147).** No Revision section (review unit): GPS article questions and active→passive rewriting (L1, p. 142); astronaut-interview main points and writing a ~60-word summary (L2, p. 143); the jobs conversation and a 50–80-word paragraph about the job one would like (L3, p. 144); invitation-conversation questions and writing two dialogues from pictures (L4, p. 145); and a two-page **Test** (pp. 146–147): reporting a comic-strip phone conversation in reported speech, five passive rewrites, and free completion of offers/invitations exchanges.

**Literature Focus exercises (pp. 148–153).** Six exercise pages keyed to the Student's Book readers: *Treasure Island* Sections 1–3 (pp. 148–150) — questions, event ordering, vocabulary-in-context, and two creative tasks (a diary entry in the voice of Ben Gunn; a synopsis for a sequel to *Treasure Island*); *Around the World in 80 Days* Sections 1–3 (pp. 151–153) — the same exercise cycle, with a warning letter from Passepartout to Fogg and a synopsis for a modern version of the story. The book ends with a ruled **Notes** page (p. 154). There is **no answer key** in the Activity Book.

### Grammar spine

The workbook practises, in the Student's Book's order: present simple and present continuous (habits, actions now, change, planned futures); indirect questions; comparison structures and adjective + infinitive; past simple, past continuous, past perfect simple and continuous; present perfect with ever/never/yet/already and the present perfect continuous; future simple and future continuous; the causative get/have something done; determiners (all/both/either/few/some); verb + to + infinitive; reported statements and questions with said/told/asked; advice modals should/ought to/could; might/might not; zero and first conditionals; adverbs of certainty; and the passive in present simple, past simple, present continuous, present perfect and future forms. The Revision and Test pages recycle the same points cumulatively, and the Unit 5 Revision adds key-word sentence transformation.

## Grade 10 — English for Iraq, 4th Preparatory Teacher's Book (Teacher's Book)

Record title it was filed under: "English for Iraq — Teacher's Guide" (Grade 10). **Verdict: the file is that book** — note the book's own name for itself is "Teacher's Book", not "Teacher's Guide".

### Identity block

- **Identity verdict vs the record:** Match in substance — the Grade 10 (4th Preparatory) teacher's book for *English for Iraq*; the record's "Teacher's Guide" wording differs from the printed "Teacher's Book".
- **Title as printed (cover and title page):** *English for Iraq — 4th Preparatory Teacher's Book*, Republic of Iraq, Ministry of Education, General Directorate of Curricula. This file, alone among the three Grade 10 volumes, **includes the front cover** (a photograph of the Ishtar Gate) and a back cover listing the course components (Student's Book, Activity Book, Teacher's Book, Audio).
- **Authors as printed:** "by Caroline de Messieres and Edward Alden" (cover and title page); Editorial and Adaptation Committee and Revision Committee as in the Student's Book.
- **Publisher:** Garnet Education / Garnet Publishing Ltd, 8 Southern Court, South Street, Reading RG1 4QS, UK (imprint, printed p. 2).
- **Edition / year as printed (imprint, printed p. 2):** First edition copyright © 2014 Garnet Publishing Ltd; **Revised edition © 2023** — the held copy is the 2023 revised edition (production code "IRQ23 TB 4thP").
- **ISBN:** No ISBN printed anywhere in this file.
- **PDF pages:** 174, all read.
- **Printed vs PDF offset:** **Printed page = PDF page − 1.** PDF p. 1 is the unnumbered front cover and PDF p. 2 the unnumbered title page (which carries a previous owner's handwritten Arabic note and phone number); the imprint is printed p. 2 (PDF p. 3), Contents printed p. 3 (PDF p. 4), and folios run unbroken to printed p. 172 (PDF p. 173, a ruled notes page); PDF p. 174 is the back cover. All page references below are to the printed pages.
- **Text-layer character:** No usable text layer — page images only; read whole from 4-up contact sheets. Printed in black and white.
- **Cast/design:** A4 teacher's book: a 12-page Introduction, then teaching notes for all eight units, each unit opening with a five-column summary table (Lesson | Materials | Topic area | Activities | Language) and closing with a per-lesson **Word list**; two appendices close the book.

### Unit-by-unit summary

**Introduction (pp. 4–15).** Section 1 introduces the New Edition (contemporary design, predictable unit structure, unit tests, integrated 21st-century skills, values/global-citizenship content, and the new Literature Focus on classic English novels) and explains the "4Cs" — Collaboration, Communication, Creativity, Critical thinking. Section 2 describes the approach: a topic-based, communicative course with a grammatical/functional syllabus and overt analysis of language structure, written for the constraints of state examinations and limited class time. Section 3 describes the components (Student's Book, Activity Book, audio, Teacher's Book) and gives the abbreviation key (SB, AB, TB, GR, track symbols). Section 4 covers organization: eight units; the presentation units have ten lessons each (nine lessons plus Round up); review Units 4 and 8 have four lessons. Section 5 details the features: unit tests and how to administer and mark them, the Grammar and Functions Reference, the Round up page, homework, dictionary training, and the gradual introduction of phonemic script (vowel sounds completed at 4th Preparatory; consonants and stress at 5th). Section 6 is advice to teachers of 16- to 18-year-olds (6.1 characteristics of young adults, 6.2 lesson planning, 6.3 recycling language, 6.4 organizing pairwork and group work, 6.5 continuous assessment, 6.6 homework and correction policy). Section 7, "Teaching in English for Iraq, 4th Preparatory", treats the four skills in turn — listening text types and sub-skills with a basic listening procedure (7.1), speaking competencies and extended turns (7.2), reading sub-skills and text types (7.3), and the writing process with its stages from brainstorming to editing (7.4). The Introduction ends with the five-column **Book map** (pp. 14–15).

**Teaching notes — Unit 1 Language learning (pp. 16–39).** Staged notes (Objectives/Language/Vocabulary box per lesson, numbered stages, homework) for Lessons 1–9, the Revision lesson and the Test lesson, with **full transcripts of Tracks 1–15, answers to every Student's Book and Activity Book exercise, and the Unit 1 Test answers** (the Pakistan text). Extras: the key-word list system for end-of-unit vocabulary/spelling tests is explained; brief use of Arabic is explicitly sanctioned for concept-checking; the Lesson 7 note directs teachers to the Unit 1 photocopiable role-play (Appendix B, p. 168). A per-lesson **Word list** closes the unit (p. 39).

**Teaching notes — Unit 2 Education (pp. 40–61).** The same apparatus for Lessons 1–9 (comparisons; UNICEF and have to; Denise's story and linking words; could/couldn't and adjective + infinitive; past simple/continuous; past perfect simple/continuous; essay sequencing; interview questions), Revision and Test (answers included), and the Unit 2 Word list (p. 61). Discussion boxes flag the children's-right-to-education theme as a values point.

**Teaching notes — Unit 3 Travel (pp. 62–83).** Lessons 1–9 (present perfect; articles with place names; checking information; timetables and the /eɪ/–/æ/ sounds; hotel requests; directions and the schwa; guidebook description; present perfect continuous), Revision, Test answers, Word list, and a blank ruled "Teacher's notes" page (p. 83).

**Teaching notes — Unit 4 Review (pp. 84–93).** Shorter notes for the four review lessons (gap year; a pilot's job; on the phone; Eid al-Adha essay writing, including the corrected version of the deliberately faulty paragraph) and the cumulative Units 1–3 Test with answers. The Unit 4 Word list (p. 93) has only four entries (gap year, antonym, co-pilot, text (v)), reflecting that the unit teaches no new language.

**Teaching notes — Unit 5 Family and friends (pp. 94–113).** Lessons 1–9 (describing people; intensifying adverbs and invitations/offers/promises; future simple; the causative, with a values box on responsible consumption; wedding customs and determiners, with guidance for the write-about-Iraqi-weddings homework; future continuous, with cultural notes on Irish names and Gaelic football; glass-blowing and strong adjectives; verb + to + infinitive; museum conjunctions/discourse markers), Revision including key-word transformation answers, Test answers, and the Unit 5 Word list (p. 113).

**Teaching notes — Unit 6 Health and fitness (pp. 114–131).** Lessons 1–9 (determiners/quantifiers; reported speech; advice modals; the doctor role-play, which uses the Unit 6 photocopiable on p. 169; the chemist's and reported speech in writing; prefixes/suffixes with the bullet-journals text; may/might; the World Cup; the exercise-and-mind article), Revision, Test answers, and the Unit 6 Word list (p. 131). Values boxes cover children's right to healthcare.

**Teaching notes — Unit 7 Science and technology (pp. 132–149).** Lessons 1–9 (science jobs; zero conditional; first conditional, with the Unit 7 photocopiable question-and-answer activity on p. 170; adverbs of certainty; the passive across tenses; summarising the online-shopping text, with a model summary printed; presentation signposting, sentence stress and pausing, including a complete sample student presentation about Jupiter; the hawksbill report and formal vocabulary), Revision, Test answers (including the Saturn reading), and the Unit 7 Word list (p. 149).

**Teaching notes — Unit 8 Review (pp. 150–157).** Notes for the four review lessons (GPS and the passive; summarising the astronaut interview, with a model summary; the jobs quiz and writing about a future job; invitation conversations) and the cumulative Units 5–8 Test with answers, plus a six-item Unit 8 Word list (p. 157).

**Appendix A — Guidance notes for the Literature Focus (pp. 158–167).** The aims of the Literature Focus (reading for pleasure, extensive reading, cultural awareness, higher-order thinking), principles for extensive reading (read "past" problem words, use the glossary, silent reading), and the spreading plan — *Treasure Island* in the first term (one section alongside each of Units 1–3) and *Around the World in 80 Days* in the second term (alongside Units 5–7) — followed by staged teaching notes and full answers for both books' Introduction pages and Sections 1–3, including the stated morals ("Cheats never prosper" for *Treasure Island*; perseverance and "Better late than never" for *Around the World in 80 Days*).

**Appendix B — Photocopiable activities (pp. 168–170).** Three classroom photocopiables, each headed "This page may be photocopied for use in the classroom only": Unit 1 Role play "Meeting for the first time" (Student A/B cards for interviewing a British tourist visiting Iraq, p. 168); Unit 6 Role play (doctor/patient advice cards, p. 169); Unit 7 Questions and answers (first-conditional question cards, p. 170). The book ends with two ruled "Teacher's notes" pages (pp. 171–172) and the back cover.

### Grammar spine

The Teacher's Book presents no grammar of its own; it operationalises the Student's Book's spine — present simple/continuous, indirect questions and imperatives; comparisons, adjective + infinitive, past simple/continuous and past perfect simple/continuous; present perfect and present perfect continuous and articles with place names; future simple and continuous, the causative, determiners and verb + to + infinitive; reported speech, advice modals and may/might; zero and first conditionals, adverbs of certainty and the passive — and its Introduction (Sections 2, 5.2 and 7) explains the course's method for teaching grammar: overt structure study inside a communicative syllabus, constant recycling, and systematic reference to the Student's Book's Grammar and Functions Reference (cited throughout the notes as GR88–97).

## Grade 11 — English for Iraq, 5th Preparatory Student's Book (Student's Book)

Record title it was filed under: "English for Iraq — Student's Book" (Grade 11). **Verdict: the file is that book**, but it is a different, earlier production from the Grade 10 set: different authors and the **original 2013 first edition**, not a 2023 revised edition.

### Identity block

- **Identity verdict vs the record:** Match — Grade 11 Student's Book of *English for Iraq*, stage name as printed "5th Preparatory" (the second year of the Iraqi preparatory stage).
- **Title as printed (cover and title page):** *English for Iraq — 5th Preparatory Student's Book*, Republic of Iraq, Ministry of Education. The front cover (a photograph of the ruins at Hatra) is included in this file.
- **Authors as printed (cover and title page):** **Olivia Johnston and Mark Farrell** — not the de Messieres/Alden team of the Grade 10 books. The Editorial and Adaptation Committee is the Ministry of Education English Curriculum Committee (Malek Mohamed Juda, Head of the English Curriculum; Adil Ali Moussa; Muayyad Naji Ahmed; Ramzi Nouri Abdullah; Batool Faeq Abdulwahid).
- **Publisher:** Garnet Education / Garnet Publishing Ltd, 8 Southern Court, South Street, Reading RG1 4QS, UK (imprint page near the end of the file, and back cover).
- **Edition / year as printed (imprint):** "**First edition, copyright © 2013** Garnet Publishing Ltd" — production code "IRQ13 SB 5thP". No revised edition is recorded in this file.
- **ISBN:** No ISBN printed; the back cover carries only a barcode with the stock number TK0702886.
- **PDF pages:** 212, all read.
- **Printed vs PDF offset:** **Printed page = PDF page − 2.** PDF p. 1 is the front cover, PDF p. 2 a blank verso, PDF p. 3 the title page; the Book Map is printed pp. 4–5 (PDF pp. 6–7 in file order: cover, verso, title, map). Unit divider pages are unnumbered but sit inside the folio sequence; the last printed folio is p. 207 (the end of the Wordlist), followed by an unnumbered imprint page, a blank page, and the back cover. All page references below are to the printed pages.
- **Text-layer character:** No text layer — page images only; read whole from 4-up contact sheets. Nearly every page carries the source website's watermark — "mlazemna.com" with Arabic "موقع ملازمنا" — in the bottom margin, showing the file was downloaded from that Iraqi study-notes site.
- **Cast/design:** Full-colour coursebook built on recurring teenage characters (Fareed, Khalid, Hadi, Samira and others) with lesson tabs cross-referencing Activity Book pages. Each presentation unit has **ten lessons plus a Round up, two Revision spreads and two Further Practice pages**; tests live in the Activity Book (each Round up footer points to "Test 1 and Test 2" Activity Book pages).

### Unit-by-unit summary

**Book Map (pp. 4–5).** The four-column syllabus grid (Listening and speaking; Reading and writing; Grammatical areas and functions; Lexical areas) for the eight units: Unit 1 Culture and customs, Unit 2 Communication, Unit 3 The environment, Unit 4 Review, Unit 5 Food and drink, Unit 6 The media, Unit 7 Leisure, Unit 8 Review — followed by the back-matter list: Grammar and Functions Reference p. 104, Literature Focus p. 114, Checklist for written work p. 122, Transcript p. 123, Wordlist p. 168.

**Unit 1 — Culture and customs (pp. 6–17).** Lesson 1 "Why are you writing to your mother?" (p. 6) is a Mother's Day dialogue revising present simple vs present continuous. Lesson 2 "Ramadan in Iraq" (p. 7) is Samira's e-mail from Basra about fasting and iftar, carrying the character-adjective set (patient, kind, generous, traditional, bossy, bad-tempered, easy-going). Lesson 3 "Have you got a problem?" (p. 8), a scooter-accident phone call, contrasts present perfect simple with past simple. Lesson 4 "A trip to Scotland" (p. 9) practises talking about experiences. Lesson 5 "You haven't met him, have you?" (p. 10) teaches question tags. Lesson 7 "What have they been doing?" (p. 11) teaches the present perfect continuous with *for* and *since*. Lesson 8 "Babylon Festival" (pp. 12–13) works with three text types (magazine article, letter, programme) about the Babylon International Festival, including an Iraqi *Hamlet* in its programme. Lesson 10 "Quiz time" (p. 14) teaches adjective + preposition patterns (good at, interested in, afraid of, proud of). The **Round up** (p. 15) recaps the unit; **Further Practice 1** (p. 16) is a tag-question interview role-play built on "The Fareed Lafta Story" (the Iraqi adventurer and skydiver), and **Further Practice 2** (p. 17) completes a rhyming poem about Ramadan and Eid.

**Unit 2 — Communication (pp. 20–31).** Lesson 1 "Keep in touch" (p. 20) covers communication technology (e-card, text message, camera phone, laptop computer) and text-message abbreviations. Lesson 2 "Plug it in and turn it on" (p. 21) teaches giving instructions with imperatives and the zero conditional, plus phrasal verbs. Lessons 3–4 "The swimmer" (pp. 22–23) assemble one story from five text types (problem page, newspaper report "Saved by a teenage hero", advertisement, diary entry, letters) about a boy who overcame a fear of water to save a drowning girl. Lesson 5 "Hold on, please" (p. 24) teaches telephone and answerphone phrases. Lesson 6 "We had such a good time" (p. 25) teaches *so* and *such* for cause and result. Lessons 8–9 "The Internet" (pp. 26–27) pair a history text ("Inventions that have changed the world") with four teenagers' accounts of their Internet use (from the UK, Lebanon, Iraq and Australia). Lesson 10 "They must have forgotten" (p. 28) teaches deductions about the past with *must have / can't have / might have* + past participle. **Round up** p. 29; **Further Practice 1** "An ancient culture" (p. 30) draws modal-perfect conclusions from an archaeologist's notes on the fictional Aloalo people; **Further Practice 2** "Buy, buy!" (p. 31) completes advertisements.

**Unit 3 — The environment (pp. 34–45).** Lesson 1 "Endangered animals" (p. 34) matches six endangered species to range maps and teaches adjective order (general adjective before colour). Lesson 2 "Almost extinct" (p. 35) gives factfiles on the North Atlantic right whale, giant panda, mountain gorilla and tiger, and teaches the first conditional. Lesson 4 "Fuad's phone" (p. 36) orders the life cycle of a mobile phone from factory to rubbish dump (lead, cadmium and mercury pollution). Lesson 5 "Now you too can be green!" (p. 37) is a recycling poster lesson teaching verbs with the prefix *re-*. Lessons 6–7 (pp. 38–39) print a Basra schoolboy's letter to a newspaper about oil and gas pollution around Rumaila and Burjussia, answered point by point by the "Green Oil & Gas Company" website. Lesson 7 "Shall I feed the baby lion?" (p. 40) teaches making and responding to offers (*Shall I …? Would you like me to …?*) with volunteers at a Serengeti reserve. Lesson 9 "The best of both worlds" (p. 41) interviews a Los Angeles driver about hybrid cars. Lesson 10 "The Arabian Oryx" (p. 42), "The oryx is back", tells how the oryx became extinct in the wild in 1972 and was reintroduced in Oman (1982) and Saudi Arabia (1990), and teaches contractions in spoken English. **Round up** p. 43; **Further Practice 1** "The Aswan Dam: Triumph or Tragedy?" (p. 44); **Further Practice 2** "Only joking" (p. 45) matches captions to cartoons.

**Unit 4 — Review of Units 1–3 (pp. 48–53).** A five-lesson review unit with no Round up or Further Practice: Lesson 1 "Bakkar's adventures" (p. 48), an article about the Egyptian Ramadan cartoon and its creator Mona Abou El Naser; Lesson 3 "Shall I help you?" (p. 49), a comic-strip dialogue; Lesson 4 "On the phone" (p. 50), listening and role-plays; Lesson 5 "I've just won first prize" (p. 51), dialogues for reacting to news and expressing feelings; Lesson 6 "Quiz time" (pp. 52–53), the personality quiz "Are you organized?".

**Unit 5 — Food and drink (pp. 56–67).** Lesson 1 "My kind of food" (p. 56) covers food vocabulary and adjectives for food (boring, cheap, delicious, quick, spicy, unhealthy, fresh). Lesson 2 "Healthy habits" (p. 57) teaches the modals *can/can't, must/mustn't, should/shouldn't*. Lesson 3 "She has to wear a uniform" (p. 58) teaches *have to* + infinitive for necessity across present, past and future. Lesson 4 "You'd better wash it first" (p. 59) teaches *had better* for strong advice in a cooking dialogue. Lessons 5–6 "If you were in trouble …" (pp. 60–61) present the **second conditional** through a magazine interview with two teenagers, Camilla of Brighton and Aziz of Baghdad. Lesson 7 "If I were you, I wouldn't worry" (p. 62) teaches asking for and giving advice. Lesson 8 "I wish I could fly" (p. 63) teaches present and future wishes (*I wish / If only* + past simple; *wish* + would/could). Lesson 9 "If only I'd listened" (pp. 64–65) is the magazine narrative "Dana's Story", used to teach past regrets (*I wish / If only* + past perfect). **Round up** p. 66; **Further Practice 2** (p. 67) is the second-conditional bravery quiz "Are you a lion or a mouse?".

**Unit 6 — The media (pp. 70–81).** Lesson 1 "What's on?" (p. 70) teaches TV programme types (cartoon, chat show, soap opera, lifestyle, weather forecast). Lesson 2 "The language of newspapers" (p. 71) has students choose the best headline for a fire report and contrasts formal written with informal spoken English. Lesson 3 "You said you were happy" (p. 72) teaches reported statements (tense and pronoun changes). Lesson 5 "Different opinions" (p. 73) compares a film poster, an audio trailer and a hostile review of the same film, *The Spanish Captain*. Lesson 6 "A famous Iraqi artist" (pp. 74–75) is an interview with the painter Widad Al Orfali (born Baghdad 1929), used to teach reported questions (with *if/whether*). Lesson 7 "Headlines" (pp. 76–77) matches four short news stories (a boy who bit a snake, Gulf golf professionals, a Thames petrol spill, a $150,000 racing camel) to their headlines and states the grammar rules of headlines (no articles, present tense, verbs often missing). Lesson 8 "At the museum" (p. 78) teaches reporting commands and requests (*tell/ask* + object + infinitive) on a school visit to the National Museum of Iraq. **Round up** p. 79; **Further Practice 1** "Only joking!" (p. 80); **Further Practice 2** "The doctor advised me to stay in bed" (p. 81) practises the wider set of reporting verbs (advise, offer, promise, remind, suggest, warn).

**Unit 7 — Leisure (pp. 84–95).** Lessons 1–2 "All the rooms are made of ice" (pp. 84–85) read an Ice Hotel (Sweden) brochure and teach the passive in the present simple, past simple and **future** (*will be* + participle). Lesson 3 "Have you ever had your eyes tested?" (p. 86) uses small advertisements to teach the causative *have/get something done*. Lesson 4 "A market where you can buy spices" (p. 87), "The Best of Iraq", describes Iraqi souqs (Al-Safafeer, Al-Souq Al-Arabi, Al-Shourja) and the southern marshes, and teaches defining relative clauses (*who, which, that, where*). Lessons 5–6 "Fifteen questions must be answered" (pp. 88–89) describe the game show *Who Wants to Be a Millionaire?* in its British and Arabic versions. Lesson 7 "Use blue or black ink" (p. 90) teaches the language of application forms over a Babylon Festival volunteer form. Lesson 9 "I'm really sorry" (p. 91) teaches apologising and making excuses, with a set of informal spoken expressions. Lesson 10 "You poor thing!" (p. 92) teaches expressing sympathy and responding to bad news. An extra reading, "Two magnificent goals" (p. 93), prints two reports of the same England–Spain football match to teach fact vs opinion in sports writing. **Round up** p. 94; **Further Practice 1** "Who was algebra invented by?" (p. 95) is a passive-voice quiz about Iraq (the Hanging Gardens of Babylon, Al Zawra Zoo, the Baghdad tower).

**Unit 8 — Review of Units 5–7 (pp. 98–103).** Six lessons: "A person who cuts hair" (p. 98), defining words with relative clauses as in a quiz show; "Wouldn't it be great!" (p. 99), a second-conditional conversation; "Caught at last!" (p. 100), a news story about an escaped boa constrictor in London, with question formation; "Travels in Arabia" (p. 101), an information-gap pair activity reporting a 90-year-old traveller's account; "What did they ask you?" (p. 102), reporting the questions of a job interview; and a Further Practice quiz, "What would you do if …? — Are you a wolf or a lamb?" (p. 103).

**Grammar and Functions Reference (pp. 104–113).** Complete form-and-use tables for every point in the book, including an extended future-forms section (will/won't, going to, may/might, adverbs of certainty, hope and expect), the full reported-speech system (statements, questions, commands/requests, and other reporting verbs), the passive in eight forms, defining and non-defining relative clauses (including *whose* and object *whom*), a table of irregular verbs, the **complete phonemic alphabet chart** (vowels and consonants, p. 113), and a list of adjectives with their prepositions.

**Literature Focus (pp. 114–121).** Two sections. **Section A: Baghdadi Bath** (pp. 114–119): an essay defining drama (tragedy and comedy; director, actors, theatre, audience), an essay on drama in Iraq from the *Epic of Gilgamesh* through the school plays of the nineteenth century to Haqqi Al-Shibli and the Institute of Fine Arts, a biography of the playwright Jawad Al-Assadi (born Baghdad 1947), a synopsis of his play *Baghdadi Bath (Hammam Baghdadi)* — two bus-driver brothers on the Baghdad–Damascus route — and one scene extract (the brothers at the Jordanian border, ordered to hand over the corpse they are carrying), with a glossary, comprehension questions and a "direct this scene" writing task. **Section B: The Tempest** (pp. 120–121): a short life of Shakespeare and introduction to the play, then the Act I Scene ii extract of Caliban's and Prospero's speeches, each followed by a modern-English paraphrase, with tasks on reading aloud and performing the extract.

**Checklist for written work (p. 122).** A one-page student checklist for planning, writing and revising, and editing (including the tip of reading a text backwards to proofread).

**Transcript (pp. 123–167).** Full transcripts of all 107 numbered Audio Sections, organised by unit, lesson, test and Further Practice.

**Wordlist (pp. 168–207).** An alphabetical list of the course's vocabulary, each item tagged with its unit and lesson (e.g. "against the law 3.2"; items marked with an asterisk are non-testable), with ruled blank lines beside every entry for students to write their own translations or notes — the list is printed **without definitions or translations**. It ends at "zakat" on p. 207. The book closes with the imprint/acknowledgements page and the back cover (course components and a barcode, no ISBN).

### Grammar spine

Revision first: present simple vs present continuous, past simple and continuous (reference), present perfect simple vs past simple, question tags, present perfect continuous with for/since, and adjective + preposition patterns (Unit 1); imperatives and the zero conditional for instructions, so/such for result, and modal-perfect deductions (must have / can't have / might have) (Unit 2); adjective order, first conditional, re- verbs, offers with Shall I / Would you like me to, future forms in full (will, going to, may/might, certainty adverbs, hope/expect) and contractions (Unit 3 and the reference section); modals of obligation and advice (can/must/should, have to, had better), the second conditional, present/future wishes and past regrets with wish/if only (Unit 5); the reported-speech system — statements, questions, commands and requests, and reporting verbs (Unit 6); the passive across present simple, past simple, present/past continuous, present perfect and future, the causative have/get something done, and defining relative clauses (Unit 7), with non-defining clauses added in the reference section. Units 4 and 8 recycle Units 1–3 and 5–7 respectively.

### Honest negatives

- **No third conditional and no mixed conditionals**: the conditional system stops at zero, first and second, plus wish/if only constructions.
- **The Wordlist gives no meanings**: it is a write-your-own glossary (headword + unit/lesson reference + blank lines), so the book contains no bilingual or defining dictionary of its own vocabulary.
- The Literature Focus is **drama only** — two play extracts with background essays; there is no prose fiction or poetry section (the only poem is the short Eid rhyme in Unit 1 Further Practice).
- This file is the **2013 first edition**, not the 2023 revised edition held for Grade 10: design, authors and lesson architecture (ten lessons + Round up + Revision + Further Practice per unit) all differ from the Grade 10 books, and some content is visibly dated (text messaging and CD-ROM-era Internet, a 2005 football report, pre-2010 game-show details).
- Copy defects of this file: the mlazemna.com website watermark on nearly every page; **no ISBN printed** (barcode stock number only); two printed pages (18 and 32) appear blank in this scan — they sit immediately before unit dividers and nothing in the surrounding folios or cross-references indicates lost content; the imprint page is placed at the end of the file rather than at the front. All 212 PDF pages are present and legible, and the printed pagination runs unbroken from p. 4 to p. 207.
