## Grade 11 — English for Iraq, 5th Preparatory Activity Book (Activity Book)

Record title it was filed under: "English for Iraq — Activity Book" (Grade 11). **Verdict: the file is that book** — but this copy is **seriously defective: 121 of its 228 PDF pages are blank** (the page images were never embedded), so the middle of the book cannot be read from this file. Details under "Copy defect" below; everything in this filing that describes unread pages is taken from the book's own Contents page and is marked as such.

### Identity block

- **Identity verdict vs the record:** Match — Grade 11 Activity Book of *English for Iraq*, stage name as printed "5th Preparatory"; the companion workbook to the Johnston/Farrell Student's Book read for this band.
- **Title as printed (cover and title page):** *English for Iraq — 5th Preparatory Activity Book*, Republic of Iraq, Ministry of Education; cover art is the same Hatra colonnade photograph as the Student's Book, tinted purple.
- **Authors as printed:** Olivia Johnston and Mark Farrell. Editorial and Adaptation Committee: Ministry of Education English Curriculum Committee (same membership as the Student's Book).
- **Publisher:** Garnet Education / Garnet Publishing Ltd, Reading, UK (imprint on PDF p. 4, facing the title page).
- **Edition / year as printed (imprint):** "First edition, copyright © 2013 Garnet Publishing Ltd" — production code "IRQ13 AB 5thP".
- **ISBN:** No ISBN printed; the back cover carries only a barcode with the stock number TK0002897.
- **PDF pages:** 228. **Pages with readable content: 107** (PDF pp. 1–76 and 198–228). **Blank pages: 121** (PDF pp. 77–197) — see the copy-defect note.
- **Printed vs PDF offset:** **Printed page = PDF page − 4** (PDF p. 1 cover, p. 2 blank, p. 3 title page, p. 4 imprint, p. 5 Contents; Unit 1 begins on printed p. 2 = PDF p. 6). The printed book runs to p. 222 (Word list) plus a blank p. 223 and the back cover. All page references below are to the printed pages.
- **Text-layer character:** No text layer — page images only; read from 4-up contact sheets. The book is printed in black and white (grayscale workbook). The mlazemna.com website watermark appears at the foot of content pages.
- **Design note:** A write-in workbook keyed page-by-page to the Student's Book by running tabs ("UNIT 1 / Lesson 3" etc.). Many pair-work speech bubbles are printed in **phonemic script** — a deliberate pronunciation device of the course (students listen and repeat), not a misprint.

### Copy defect — read this first

PDF pp. 77–197 are **completely blank white pages**. This was verified three ways: the page renders are byte-identical empty images; a fresh 150-DPI render of a sample page is equally blank; and the PDF's own structure reports the image objects for those pages as missing ("XObject unknown" errors), i.e. the scans were never embedded in this file. In printed-page terms the lost run is **printed pp. 73–193**, which by the book's Contents (p. 1 of the front matter; unit starts: U1 p. 2, U2 p. 27, U3 p. 54, U4 p. 80, U5 p. 100, U6 p. 136, U7 p. 166, U8 p. 199, Word list p. 220) covers: the end of Unit 3 (its Test 2 and Further Practice, pp. 73–79), **the whole of Unit 4** (pp. 80–99), **the whole of Unit 5** (pp. 100–135), **the whole of Unit 6** (pp. 136–165), and Unit 7 from its start to mid-Test 2 (pp. 166–192). **This book has therefore NOT been read whole**; a complete copy is needed to finish the filing. What follows covers only the pages that exist in this file.

### Unit-by-unit summary (readable pages only)

**Unit 1 — Culture and customs, exercises (pp. 2–26; read in full).** Lesson exercises mirror the Student's Book point by point: present simple vs present continuous choice and sentence writing (Lesson 1); the character adjectives with an explicit bilingual step — "How do you say the words in Arabic? Check in a dictionary and write the translation" (p. 4) — plus a guided paragraph "describing a friend" (p. 5); present perfect simple vs past simple with the two forms labelled PS/PPS (pp. 6–7); *been/gone* (p. 7); question tags, including pair role-plays and sentence-half matching built on Iraqi content (Babylon City, Al Kindi, Fairouz) (pp. 8–10); present perfect continuous with *for/since* and a holiday-camp postcard (p. 11); Babylon Festival vocabulary and /p/–/b/ minimal-pair listening (pp. 12–13); written e-mail tasks (p. 13); adjective + preposition completion (p. 14). **Revision 1** (pp. 14–16) is a writing project: reorder the paragraphs of a model essay about the Notting Hill Carnival, plan an essay on a festival or celebration of the student's own, and write 100–150 words. **Revision 2** (pp. 16–17) is a vocabulary crossword whose answers are given as anagrams (the grid itself is on a separate sheet the teacher provides). **Test 1** (pp. 18–20): Babylon Festival listening leaflet, character adjectives, present simple/continuous matching, question tags, present perfect vs past simple dialogue. **Test 2** (pp. 21–22): reorder a Scottish girl's letter about New Year in Glasgow (kilts, bagpipes, "Auld Lang Syne", New Year resolutions), answer questions on it, and write about 150 words on a traditional celebration in Iraq. **Further Practice 1** (pp. 23–24): the Student B half of the Fareed Lafta role-play and completion of the Eid al Fitr dialogue. **Further Practice 2** (pp. 25–26): three extra verses for the Eid poem, phonemic-script reading (e.g. /fa:st/, /swi:t/), and vowel pronunciation sets.

**Unit 2 — Communication, exercises (pp. 27–53; read in full).** Compound nouns (phone game, text message, birthday card; hair dryer, washing machine) and text-message decoding ("R U OK?", "C U L8R!", "Talk 2 U L8R!") (pp. 27–28); instruction language matched to pictures (click, plug in, press, select, set, switch on/off) and sentence stress (pp. 29–30); completing machine instructions (mouse, washing machine, remote control, cash machine, photo booth) and writing instructions for a machine (pp. 31–32); a linking-word summary of the swimmer story and a letter about the rescue (pp. 32–33); answerphone-message questions (p. 34); *so/such … that* (pp. 35–36); suggestions and linking sounds in connected speech (pp. 36–37); Internet vocabulary, questions on the invention text, pie-chart matching and agreeing/disagreeing/interrupting phrase boxes (pp. 38–39); modal-perfect deductions (pp. 40–41). **Revision 1** (pp. 42–43) is data commentary: a table and bar chart of UK mobile-phone use in 2001 and 2003 are turned into a completed report, then the same is done for UK cinema attendance. **Revision 2** (pp. 44–45) practises vocabulary through vowel-less words and missing first/last letters. **Test 1** (pp. 45–47): Palm Trees Sports Club listening notes, past simple/past continuous sentence writing, zero conditional, *so/such*. **Test 2** (pp. 48–51): a gapped article "Mobile Phones" (from the 1946 Swedish police phone to 1.5 billion users and the 2004 tsunami rescues) and sentence completion from a UK cinema-attendance table and graph, 1992–2002. **Further Practice 1** (p. 51): pair conversations making deductions about strange sights. **Further Practice 2** (pp. 52–53): invent a product name and write an advertisement using an imperative and a zero conditional; pronunciation of vowel combinations.

**Unit 3 — The environment, exercises (pp. 54–72 read; pp. 73–79 BLANK in this copy).** Adjective-order phrase building ("an old black and white bus") and describing animals (p. 54); factfile scanning and *look / look like* (pp. 55–56); first conditional joining with two-clause intonation (p. 57); the mobile-phone life-cycle flow chart (p. 58); the full text of "Why we should recycle" by Professor Khaled Al Jaber as a cloze (p. 59) with *re-* verb matching and a poster-design task (p. 60); the Green Oil & Gas Company's reply letter as a cloze and a discussion of how Basra has changed (p. 61); offers listen-and-repeat (p. 62); checking understanding — labelling a listener's replies as extra question (EQ) or different words (DW) (pp. 62–63); hybrid-car advantages table and a "Which car to buy" interview (p. 64); a model problem–solution essay, "How can we solve the problem of overfishing?", mined for linking phrases before students write the parallel essay on car pollution (p. 65); oryx true/false and contractions written out in full and spoken contracted (p. 66). **Revision 1** (pp. 67–68): reorder a for/against hunting essay, identify paragraph functions, brainstorm "Zoos – for and against" and write the essay in 100–150 words. **Revision 2** (p. 69): a word maze. **Test 1** (pp. 70–72): "The Mangasha National Reserve" listening factfile, *re-* verbs, checking-understanding replies, first conditional, *will/shall*. **Not readable:** Test 2 and Further Practice 1–2 (printed pp. 73–79 are blank in this file).

**Units 4, 5 and 6 (printed pp. 80–165) — NOT READABLE in this copy.** All 86 pages are blank. From the Contents, Unit 4 (the review of Units 1–3) starts at p. 80, Unit 5 (Food and drink) at p. 100 and Unit 6 (The media) at p. 136; by the pattern of Units 1–3 and 7–8 each will contain lesson exercises keyed to the Student's Book, two Revision spreads, Test 1, Test 2 and Further Practice pages — but none of that content exists in this file, and nothing about it is asserted here beyond the Contents page itself.

**Unit 7 — Leisure (printed pp. 166–192 BLANK in this copy; pp. 193–198 read).** The only surviving Unit 7 pages are the end of **Test 2** — a guided story plan, "A shopping trip that went wrong" (p. 193) — **Further Practice 1** (p. 194), which simply prints the answers to the Student's Book p. 95 passive quiz (Al Khawarizmi; around 600 BCE; Al Jahshiyari; Baghdad Tower; London 2012; Orry; the 9th century; 1971, reopened 2003) and asks students to write five more passive quiz questions, and **Further Practice 2** (pp. 195–198), a story-expansion exercise: a desert-trip skeleton text full of prompts is copied out and fleshed out with details, then a second story, "a trip with some kind of problem", is written to a three-paragraph plan in about 150 words. The unit's lesson exercises, Revision spreads and Test 1 are in the blank run.

**Unit 8 — Review, exercises (pp. 199–218; read in full).** Lesson 1 (pp. 199–201): write definitions using relative pronouns ("A competitor is someone who takes part in a competition or game"), then split A/B pair pages (p. 200 for Student A, p. 201 for Student B) for a department-store dialogue in which students describe unnamed objects (mask, stapler, pram, kettle, binoculars…) and are told the word and department. Lesson 2 (p. 202): complete the "Ahlen Productions" film-extras newspaper advertisement from a radio advert, and write a self-description for an audition. Lesson 3 (p. 203): a passive-voice cloze about abandoned pet snakes, then inventing stories from passive headlines ("ALLIGATOR FOUND IN FLORIDA SWIMMING POOL", "MOBILE STOLEN BY MONKEY", "Valuable Camel Stolen") and writing a 60-word news report. Lesson 4 (pp. 204–206): the Arabia traveller information-gap in reported speech (two versions of the 90-year-old's account, 1945 vs 1946 — spot the differences), reporting ten spoken messages with tense changes, a five-things list game, and reporting what others said. Lesson 5 (pp. 207–208): convert twelve interview questions into reported questions, then play the group game "What's my job?", in which a chosen job (from a 20-job list) is guessed from the reported questions. **Revision** (pp. 209–211): personal second-conditional completions, seven-line advice dialogues, and "You'd better … because …" sentence building. **Test 1** (pp. 211–214): a TV-guide listening (including the dolphin film), identifying reporting-verb functions (advising, offering, promising, reminding, suggesting, warning), vocabulary completion and odd-one-out, and a 15-item multiple-choice grammar test spanning Units 5–7 (had better, second conditional, wishes and regrets, have to, reported commands and questions, passives, relative pronouns, causative have). **Test 2** (pp. 215–217): a reading text on rice growing and its traditional uses, rich in passives, with topic ticking, true/false/doesn't-say, finding passive examples in the text, and a 100-word story, "A very difficult day". **Further Practice** (p. 218): the scoring key for the Student's Book "wolf or lamb" quiz and writing three more quiz questions.

**Word list (pp. 220–222; read).** Unlike the Student's Book's alphabetical list, the Activity Book's word list is organised **by unit** (Units 1–7 in three-column format), each word followed only by its unit-and-lesson reference (key explained on p. 220: "1.2 = Unit 1, Lesson 2"), with no definitions or translations. **It stops at Unit 7 — there is no Unit 8 section.**

### Grammar spine (of the readable pages)

The workbook adds no grammar presentation of its own; it drills the Student's Book sequence: present simple vs continuous, present perfect simple vs past simple, question tags, present perfect continuous (Unit 1); imperatives, zero conditional, so/such, past simple vs continuous, modal-perfect deductions (Unit 2); adjective order, first conditional, re- verbs, checking understanding, contractions (Unit 3); and in Unit 8 a consolidation of the second half of the course — defining relative clauses, the passive, reported statements/questions/commands, reporting verbs, second conditional, had better, wishes/regrets and the causative. The grammar of Units 4–6 cannot be characterised from this copy because those pages are blank.

### Honest negatives

- **The dominant fact about this file: it is incomplete.** Printed pp. 73–193 (PDF pp. 77–197) are blank — Units 4, 5 and 6 entire, most of Unit 7, and Unit 3's Test 2/Further Practice are absent. Any statement about those pages in this filing comes only from the Contents page. A complete copy of IRQ13 AB 5thP is required before this book can be counted as read.
- Of the readable pages: the workbook contains **no grammar explanations** (rules live in the Student's Book's Grammar and Functions Reference) and **no answer key** — the single exception is Further Practice 1 of Unit 7 (p. 194), which prints the answers to the Student's Book quiz.
- The Word list gives **no definitions or Arabic translations** and omits Unit 8 entirely — an apparent production slip in the book itself, visible on the surviving pages.
- The Revision 2 crossword in Unit 1 cannot be completed from the book alone: its grid is on a separate sheet "your teacher gives you" (p. 16).
- Otherwise the readable pages are clean: pagination is continuous across the blank run (printed p. 72 is followed, after the blanks, by p. 193 in the correct Unit 7 Test 2 position), the mlazemna.com watermark is the only overlay, and no ISBN is printed (barcode stock number TK0002897 only).

## Grade 12 — English for Iraq, 6th Preparatory Teacher's Book (Teacher's Book)

Record title it was filed under: "English for Iraq — Teacher's Guide" (Grade 12). **Verdict: the book is the Grade 12 Teacher's Book, but the record's name for the kind of book is wrong in the same way as at Grades 10 and 11 — the book prints "Teacher's Book", not "Teacher's Guide".** A second, bigger difference the record does not show: the author team is different from the Student's and Activity Books (see the identity block).

### Identity block

- **Identity verdict vs the record:** Match as to book and grade — the 6th Preparatory teacher's companion to *English for Iraq* — with the standing record slip: "Guide" for printed "Book".
- **Title as printed:** *English for Iraq — 6th Preparatory Teacher's Book*, Republic of Iraq, Ministry of Education. This copy has **no separate cover**: the file opens directly on the title page, which carries the series title in the standard display type, the ministry seal, the Editorial and Adaptation Committee box (Ministry of Education English Curriculum Committee — Malek Mohamed Juda, Head of the English Curriculum; Adil Ali Moussa; Muayyad Naji Ahmed; Ramzi Nouri Abdullah; Batool Faeq Abdulwahid) and the Garnet logo.
- **Authors as printed:** **Caroline de Messieres, Neil Blacknell and Lyn Edwards** (title page). Note the split across the Grade 12 set: the Student's and Activity Books are by Olivia Johnston and Caroline de Messieres; only de Messieres carries over to this book, joined by Blacknell and Edwards.
- **Publisher:** Garnet Publishing Ltd, 8 Southern Court, South Street, Reading RG1 4QS, UK.
- **Edition / year as printed:** "First edition, copyright © 2013 Garnet Publishing Ltd" — production code "IRQ13 TB 6thP" (imprint, PDF p. 2), with the same ministry thanks list as the companion books (Sajeda Naser Khudair, Faten Fakhri Khalaf, Eman Selman Farhan, Hala Talib Hammood).
- **ISBN:** No ISBN printed. This copy also has **no back cover** (see Honest negatives), so no barcode stock number can be recorded for this book.
- **PDF pages:** 180, all read.
- **Printed vs PDF offset:** printed page = PDF page − 2 throughout (title page and imprint unnumbered; Introduction begins on printed p. 2 = PDF p. 4; the book ends on printed p. 178 = PDF p. 180). Printed folios run 2–178.
- **Text-layer character:** A real, usable OCR text layer from the Canon scan (Adobe Acrobat 10 Paper Capture, created September 2013) — light OCR noise throughout (broken words, "RG1" read as "RGl" in the imprint, the odd garbled answer key), but legible on every page. The book was read whole from this text layer, with the title page, imprint and contents also checked visually on the contact sheets.
- **Design:** The standard *English for Iraq* teacher's-book architecture, unchanged from Grade 11: an Introduction (methodology, course organisation, skills guidance), Teaching Notes for all eight units (a unit summary table first — Lesson / Materials / Topic-Title / Objectives / Language — then staged, timed lesson notes with full answers, transcripts printed inline, homework lines keyed to Activity Book pages, and a unit word list), and five appendices.

### Introduction (pp. 2–13)

**§1 The course.** The book presents 6th Preparatory as the final book in the 12-year cycle, designed for young adults and giving "clear preparation for the final examination"; its topics fall under two heads — life outside school and life after school (university study or future careers) — and its emphasis is on English as an international language. **§2 Approach to teaching.** A communicative approach whose principal aim is communicative competence, combined — as introduced at the Secondary stage — with a **more overt analysis of the structure of the language**, justified three ways: students this age can understand the concepts behind the rules; analysis recycles earlier language for accuracy and fluency; and the state examination requires students to demonstrate knowledge of language as well as ability to use it. Mistakes are treated as part of the developmental process; a self-study strand runs through the course, building on the learner diaries and vocabulary notebooks taught in 4th and 5th Preparatory. **§3 Components of the course.** Student's Book (presentation texts — articles, e-mails, advertisements, leaflets, interviews, questionnaires — plus the Grammar and Functions Reference); Activity Book (consolidation, examination tips, and the Test yourself activities "specifically designed to help the students revise for the examination"); Audio — with the explicit note that **6th Preparatory has less focus on listening than the previous two grades, in line with the demands of the final examination**; and this Teacher's Book, whose per-lesson box (materials, aims, language, vocabulary) and staged, timed notes are explained, along with the abbreviation system (SB, AB, TB, GR, T, S, C, G). **§4 Organization.** Eight topic units; Units 1–3 and 5–7 revise and develop earlier language, while **Units 4 and 8 focus on revision in preparation for the final examinations at the ends of the two semesters**. Every unit follows the same pattern: eight core lessons, one Test yourself lesson, and one reading extension lesson — the last lesson being non-core material that can be omitted if time is short. The Introduction closes with a Book Map-style grid of all eight units under Listening and speaking, Reading and writing, Grammatical areas and functions, Lexical areas, and Language learning. **§5 Features of the course.** §5.1 is devoted to the Test yourself lessons (Activity Book Lesson 9): they are practice under exam-like conditions rather than strict tests — students may consult each other; they test **vocabulary, grammar and writing only, with no direct testing of reading**; the teacher should collect the Activity Books, mark them, keep a written record of each student's performance, and review problem areas with the class before handing the books back. §5.2 introduces the Grammar and Functions Reference. **§6 Advice to teachers of 6th Preparatory.** Planning lessons at least a day ahead; using the stage timings as guidelines, not rules; realia and authentic materials; §6.3 recycling language, with routines listed for social language and question tags, opinions, directions, describing experiences, a range of tenses, timetables, and imperatives and polite requests (including lend and borrow); §6.4 organising pairwork and group work (explain before splitting the class; group students of similar ability together as a rule; use a regular attention signal); §6.5 continuous assessment; and feedback on written work that highlights errors for self-correction rather than correcting them outright, with homework set clearly and deadlines made explicit. **§7 Teaching in English for Iraq, 6th Preparatory.** The four skills in turn: 7.1 Listening (text types; a list of sub-skills; a set procedure — prepare, play, compare in pairs, elicit, confirm — and the standing note that **listening tasks should not be used as a test** of listening ability); 7.2 Speaking (a competency list for the year, discussion and problem-solving work, and extended turns); 7.3 Reading (the widest possible range of text types, silent reading as the norm, and the Literature Focus as extended reading for pleasure — with the placement ruling that **Section A is taught after Unit 3 and Section B after Unit 7**, at the teacher's discretion); and the corresponding writing guidance carried through the feedback advice.

### Teaching notes (pp. 14–153)

**Unit 1 — The health service (pp. 14–33).** Summary table from SB p. 6 / AB p. 2 ("My toe is bleeding") onward; staged notes for the ten lessons with answers to every Student's and Activity Book exercise, transcripts for the A&E dialogue and the shark article's audio work, homework lines, guidance for running the Lesson 9 Test yourself and for the Lesson 10 Mustafa reading, and the unit word list.

**Unit 2 — The police and armed forces (pp. 34–51).** The same apparatus for the law-and-order unit: the summary table opens with "Law and order" (SB16/AB26) and "A police officer's duties", and the Language column is headed throughout by *Revision* — used to and the past tenses from Unit 1, then the modals of necessity, obligation, lack of obligation and prohibition, modal sentences for polite requests, suggestions, offers and advice, and should/shouldn't for expectation, with common telephone phrases listed as a language item in their own right.

**Unit 3 — Vocational training (pp. 52–71).** Notes for the conditional unit: the Language column works through revision of the zero, first and second conditionals, reported speech and question forms; answers and transcripts for the careers interviews and the Zaha Hadid lesson; guidance for the regret and third-conditional lessons, the Birchwood College lessons (which sit in the Activity Book), the Test yourself, and the Samira interpreting reading.

**Unit 4 — The world of business (pp. 72–83).** The first revision unit gets a shorter note set matching its seven lessons: the summary table's Language column is entirely revision (past simple, past continuous, used to, have to, indirect speech, I wish/If only, the third conditional, and language to describe personality). Notes cover both books' lessons, including the Activity-Book-only Lesson 7 with the Dahab and MARHABABA application letters.

**Unit 5 — Holidays (pp. 84–103).** Notes for the perfect-system unit: compound nouns, the present perfect simple and continuous with ever/never and for/since, How long …? questions, the past perfect, and relative clauses, with the Language learning strand named in the table (putting vocabulary into groups; choosing the correct tense to convey meaning); answers for the brainstorming and essay lessons, the long Test yourself (the heaviest test in the course), and the Lesson 10 holidays essay reading.

**Unit 6 — Banking and finance (pp. 104–123).** Notes for the passive and conditionals unit: decoding the bank statement, the account-types lesson, the banker text (with the main-idea work answered), the four problem conversations with their conditional analysis, the formal/informal letter lessons and Samira's complaint, the Activity-Book-only Lesson 8, the Test yourself, and the Lesson 10 saving-and-investing reading.

**Unit 7 — Further education (pp. 124–141).** Notes for the future-forms unit: the five-way future classification, the computer-skills article and the inference work (with the inference reasoning modelled for the teacher), the catalogue and future-in-the-past lessons, the Linking ideas and Expressing opinions lessons, the staged distance-learning essay of Activity Book Lesson 8, the Test yourself, and the Lesson 10 library text. The unit's Language learning strand names "understanding making inferences" and "practising persuasive language".

**Unit 8 — The environment and recycling (pp. 142–153).** The second revision unit: a seven-lesson table (Lessons 1–5, the Lesson 6 Test yourself, Lesson 7) whose Language column revises future forms (in Lesson 1's dialogue work), the passive for process description, conditionals, and the future in the past inside the test; notes for the wind-power comparison lessons, the environment-jobs listening, the global-warming essay build, and the river fact-file lesson.

### Appendices (pp. 154–178)

**Appendix A: Classroom activities and games (pp. 154–156).** A games bank in the series' standard families: vocabulary games (a letter/sound team game; a picture memory game; a back-to-the-class describing game in which the team "cannot use Arabic"; a draw-it-line-by-line game) and word recognition and spelling games (the missing-word game, board anagrams, a spelling race). **Appendix B: Glossary (pp. 157–160).** Definitions of the teaching terms used in the notes — as printed, the entries begin at "Check understanding" (there are no A–B entries), and run through "Closed pairs", "Demonstrate", "Digraph" and the rest of the standard list. **Appendix C: Complete alphabetical word list (pp. 161–163).** Every word of the year with its unit.lesson reference; words marked with an asterisk are non-testable ("a risky business * 6.10"). **Appendix D: Transcripts (pp. 164–176).** The full audio scripts, headed by unit and lesson and audio section ("Unit 1 Lesson 1, Section 1" onwards), duplicating the transcripts printed inline in the teaching notes. **Appendix E: Guidance notes for Literature Focus (p. 177).** One page: the four learning objectives of the Literature Focus (reading for pleasure above all; a broader, literary and metaphorical spectrum of language; awareness of other cultures; higher-order and critical thinking), then the answers for Section A, "The Swing" (the factual key includes that the soldier must tell the family his friend died in battle, that Iraq was at war with Israel, and the hot loaf of bread), and for Section B, "The Canary" (including the synonym-matching key) — most exercises are "Students' own answers", with handling advice to elicit and discuss before writing. **Notes (p. 178).** A blank notes page closes the book.

### Grammar spine

The Teacher's Book teaches no grammar of its own; it documents and stages the Student's Book spine, and its summary tables label almost every Grade 12 grammar item **"Revision"** — the honest picture of the exam year: past simple/continuous and used to (Unit 1); the modal system of obligation, prohibition, advice and expectation (Unit 2); the four conditionals, wish/if only and reported questions (Unit 3); the perfect system and relative clauses (Unit 5); the passive and the conditional system systematised (Unit 6); the five future forms and future in the past (Unit 7); with Units 4 and 8 given over entirely to revision of the whole. The Introduction's own framing is that the grammatical syllabus "revises and extends" what the students have already studied, and that overt analysis of structure is itself part of developing communicative competence at this stage.

### Honest negatives

- **This copy has no back cover and no front cover.** The file opens on the title page and ends on the printed Notes page (p. 178); there is no barcode or stock number anywhere in it, and no ISBN is printed on the imprint either — this book's stock number cannot be recorded from this copy (the Student's Book copy carries TK0002689 on its back cover).
- The record name "Teacher's Guide" is wrong for the whole *English for Iraq* series as filed: all three Grade 12 books' companions at Grades 10 and 11 print "Teacher's Book", and so does this one.
- The OCR text layer, while usable, is noisy: expect broken words on most pages and occasional garbling in dense answer keys (the Literature Focus matching key in Appendix E reads with letter/digit confusions). Any quotation taken from this layer should be checked against the page image.
- The Teacher's Book contains no separate answer-key section and no photocopiable pages of its own in this edition: answers are carried inside the staged lesson notes, and the tests' answers likewise (the tests themselves live in the Activity Book).
- Appendix E's Literature Focus guidance is a single page and mostly "Students' own answers" — the book offers far less support for the two stories than its lesson-by-lesson support for the units.
